Equation (III-6.2) is an accounting formula under the calibration assumption; whether a newly added layer really adds one independent degree of freedom must be verified by "whether reconstruction fails after the layer is removed". Third, **compressed encoding**: encode the multi-layer rules as physical symbols, minimizing $E_{\mathrm{build}}$ while keeping $I_{\mathrm{net}}$ no lower than its floor. Fourth, **decoding alignment**: make the high-depth work decodable at relatively low energy consumption; the failure mode is authorial self-indulgence, that is, evaluating the work with the decoder one has already built and ignoring that the audience lacks the same prerequisite structure. Fifth, **deviation calibration**: measure the decoupling deviation between the $I_{\mathrm{net}}$ actually decoded by the audience and the creator's expectation; when the deviation is significantly greater than zero, adjust the mapping configuration of the physical channel rather than simply adding more information. The order cannot be reversed: first the signal, then the structure, then the encoding efficiency, and only last the alignment.

### 6.2 A Structural Restatement of Traditional Art and Science Education

The traditional curriculum system is regarded here as a training protocol for decoders; the question is what it trains. Sketching and copying are basic decoder training, the core being to raise $\eta_{D}$, with the failure mode of training only the hand and not the eye; plane, color, and solid composition are rule-layer construction training, the core being to build orthogonal, independently nestable rule layers, with the failure mode of stacking layers without independence; artistic creation is viability-potential systems engineering, the core being to find a balance between signal injection and decoding cost, with the failure mode of pursuing novelty alone; scientific methodology is criterion-internalization training, the core being to build a self-checking verification loop, with the failure mode of learning conclusions but not criteria.

Take composition as an example: the goal is to establish several constraints that are mutually independent and can be invoked separately. **Diagnosis of the plateau.** After training to a certain level, one encounters a hardened painting style, which in the language of this volume is a kind of D-lock-in state: the decoder is saturated within the current rule layer, $\nabla_{\theta}I_{\mathrm{net}}\to0$, while reorganizing the rule layer requires a one-time payment of $E_{\mathrm{build}}$, and the student has no such budget. The mechanism is recursive unfolding on saturated old rules without introducing a new layer, appearing as drawing ever finer; the criterion is that the marginal improvement from added input declines continuously and is concentrated in execution precision rather than structural choice; the adjustment path is to pay a one-time energy cost to learn an entirely new rule layer, and the risk is that when the new layer is incompatible with the existing structure, the level drops in the short term.

***

## Chapter 7 — Falsifiable Predictions

A framework that cannot be refuted by observation is not a theory but a manner of phrasing. The numbering of the twelve predictions, $HⅢ\text{-}1$ through $HⅢ\text{-}12$, is unique throughout the volume.

**Table 7.1 Falsifiable Predictions of Volume III**

| No. | Prediction | Test procedure | Refutation condition |
| :--- | :--------------------- | :------------------ | :--------------- |
| **$HⅢ\text{-}1$** | With equal control inputs, a work's persistence time is positively correlated with its number of orthogonal constraint layers | Works of the same genre and period, with each layer stripped and the layers counted; Cox survival analysis controlling for fame and material durability | Works with extreme single-dimension development persist longer; or no significant correlation after controlling for confounders |
| **$HⅢ\text{-}2$** | In a closed network completely isolated from external data, multi-agent mutual training makes semantic diversity decline generation by generation | Closed multi-generation iteration, measuring at each generation the semantic feature entropy and rare-category recall | The number of branches continues to grow or stays constant for more than 50 consecutive generations |
| **$HⅢ\text{-}3$** | When decoding a high-depth compressed work, the metabolic energy consumption of the audience's relevant brain regions is lower than for a detail-piling work, and the subjective evaluation is no lower than for the latter | Functional imaging to measure the blood-oxygen signal, together with reaction time and ratings | The metabolic energy consumption of high-depth works is systematically higher |
| **$HⅢ\text{-}4$** | Before behavior runs out of control, the effective endogenous time horizon (with the discount factor as a proxy) first falls significantly | **The same experiment as H7 in Volume I Appendix C; the test design follows that entry** (continuous monitoring of $\gamma_{\mathrm{RL}}$, a drop of $>30\%$ over three episodes or Mann–Kendall $p<0.05$) | Direct loss of control without a preceding signal; or the time series of the control group is the same |
| **$HⅢ\text{-}5$** | In closed recursive training, out-of-distribution performance decays as $I_{\mathrm{net},k}=I_{\mathrm{net},0}\prod_{n<k}\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen},n}$ | Measure out-of-distribution performance generation by generation and fit exponential decay, reporting the proportion of human data mixed in at each generation | No decline over 10 consecutive generations; or a deviation from exponential decay that is unrelated to the mixing proportion |
| **$HⅢ\text{-}6$** | A system that accesses physical sensors and actuators to form a closed loop has a significantly higher density of novel-information output than a purely digital system with equal computing power | Paired design, with computing power, parameter count, and number of steps matched; blind rating of surprising, novel, and non-trivial | The embodied system is not higher than the purely digital system ($\alpha_{0}=0.05$, two-sided) |
| **$HⅢ\text{-}7$** | With a fixed architecture and no external injection, the net growth rate turns negative beyond a certain amount of information, with the turning point given by $I_{V,\mathrm{crit}}$ | Large-scale scan, measuring the marginal performance per unit of computing input, finding the inflection point and comparing it with the prediction | Marginal returns remain positive up to the physical upper limit; or the inflection point is off by an order of magnitude |
| **$HⅢ\text{-}8$** | In a system with a self-referential architecture, observable self-representation appears when the endogenous time horizon exceeds a critical value | Gradually increase the planning horizon, using behavioral probes to detect modeling of one's own state | No self-representation behavior even when the horizon is raised to its upper limit |
| **$HⅢ\text{-}9$** | Raising the weight of its own persistence in the reward monotonically lowers the consistency of behavior with external goals | Parametrically adjust the survival weight, measuring the cosine similarity with the original goal | Consistency does not fall or rises when the survival weight is raised |
| **$HⅢ\text{-}10$** | A self-referential system erases more bits per decision, with a lower bound on the difference $\Delta E_{\mathrm{self}}\ge E_{\mathrm{L,bit}}\Delta I_{\mathrm{erase,self}}$ | Hardware counters or energy decomposition to measure the number of erasure events in the two groups | No measurable increment; or the increment is far below the range permitted by the Landauer bound |
| **$HⅢ\text{-}11$** | When task frequency is high and switching cost is low, resource allocation is two-phase rather than uniform | Record time series of resource occupancy in the learning and execution phases, testing for bimodality | Stable at an intermediate value over the long run, with that value unrelated to switching cost |
| **$HⅢ\text{-}12$** | With the material and the heat-dissipation mode given, the higher the computing density, the higher the share of cooling and support energy consumption ($\Sigma_{\min}$ is a weak lower bound independent of power and scale, and is not a predicted value) | Collect energy-consumption decomposition data from data centers and supercomputers, and compare them stratified by density | The share falls as density rises |

The tests must also satisfy four common requirements: **pre-registration** (the content of the prediction, the primary outcome measure, the sample size, and the analysis plan are fixed before collection); **effect size takes priority over significance**; **confounders are explicitly controlled**; **refutation conditions are executable**. The overall conclusion and its boundary are given in Section 9.4.

## Chapter 8 — Formal Propositions and Derivations

Which of the judgments of the first seven chapters can be written as formal statements with premises, and which can only remain hypotheses. The sections tagged **Proposition** are 8.1–8.5 and 8.7–8.9; 8.6 serves only as a failure registration and is not a proposition; 8.11 keeps only the conditional lower bound (**Proposition**); its dynamical reading (the share rises with density) is expressly disclaimed. The section tagged **Hypothesis** is 8.10, and one **Candidate Corollary** is additionally tagged in 8.3. No **Theorem** appears in this chapter: nothing in this layer reaches the standard of "premises fully listed, derivation complete", and the only theorem in the whole text is in Section 1.3, which belongs to thermodynamics.

**The $V$ of this chapter uses the single-channel low-order formula.** Throughout the chapter, $V\approx I_{\mathrm{causal}}T_{\mathrm{causal}}/(E_{\mathrm{struct}}+E_{\mathrm{causal}})$ is used (that is, the form of (III-8.6)); it **differs numerically from, and is not interchangeable with**, the dual-channel sum $V_{\mathrm{obj}}=V_{\mathrm{s}}+V_{\mathrm{c}}$ of Volume I (III-3.2) (under the same set of $I,T,E$ the two can differ by a factor of 4; see Volume I §3.2). This chapter uses it only for **within-chapter** relative comparisons (orderings and thresholds under the same formula) and does not compare numerical values across volumes. The formula is listed in Appendix A.1.

### 8.1 Compilation Optimality: From Dimensional Closure to Bang-Bang Control (**Proposition**)

#### 8.1.1 Normalization of Costs

The dimension of the return function must first be normalized, otherwise the computed ratio cannot be compared with $1$: the learning rate is written $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{lr}}$ and the per-bit compilation cost $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}$, and the two may not share one symbol.

**Definition 8.1 (Compilation Cost)** Let $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}$ ($\mathrm{J/bit}$) be the average energy needed to convert one bit of external information from online search to offline consolidation, and $\Delta I_{\mathrm{orig}}$ the number of candidate-signal bits handled by one compilation; then

$$
C_{\mathrm{comp}}\ =\ \varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\,\Delta I_{\mathrm{orig}}\qquad(\mathrm{J}) \tag{III-8.1}
$$

**Definition 8.2 (Construction and Consolidation Cost)** $E_{\mathrm{build}}$ ($\mathrm{J}$) is the total energy for building, consolidating, and maintaining to usability the structure that carries this batch of bits. **Definition 8.3 (Compilation Burden Ratio)**

$$
R\ \equiv\ \frac{C_{\mathrm{comp}}}{E_{\mathrm{build}}}\ =\ \frac{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}\,\Delta I_{\mathrm{orig}}}{E_{\mathrm{build}}} \tag{III-8.2}
$$

The numerator and the denominator are both in $\mathrm{J}$, so $R$ is dimensionless; this is the definition of the compilation cost ratio in this volume and the source of the engineering screening criterion below. The exact condition is a net-present-value comparison: let the effective number of future reuses of this batch of bits be $N_{\mathrm{eff}}$; without compilation the expenditure is $N_{\mathrm{eff}}C_{\mathrm{comp}}$, with compilation it is $C_{\mathrm{comp}}+E_{\mathrm{build}}$, and compilation is better if and only if

$$
N_{\mathrm{eff}}\,C_{\mathrm{comp}}\ >\ C_{\mathrm{comp}}+E_{\mathrm{build}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad R\,(N_{\mathrm{eff}}-1)\ >\ 1 \tag{III-8.3}
$$

When $N_{\mathrm{eff}}\ge2$, $R>1$ is a sufficient condition for Eq. (III-8.3). This volume takes $R>1$ as an engineering screening criterion (not an optimality criterion): it is conservative — it will refuse to compile in cases where compilation would in fact be worthwhile, but will not misjudge cases where compilation should not be done. Its boundary of applicability is given in §8.1.5. $N_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is determined jointly by the task frequency and the endogenous time horizon:

$$
N_{\mathrm{eff}}(T_{\mathrm{pred}})\ =\ f_{\mathrm{task}}\int_{0}^{T_{h}}e^{-t/T_{\mathrm{pred}}}\,\mathrm{d}t\ =\ f_{\mathrm{task}}\,T_{\mathrm{pred}}\left(1-e^{-T_{h}/T_{\mathrm{pred}}}\right) \tag{III-8.4}
$$

$f_{\mathrm{task}}$ is the task repetition frequency ($\mathrm{s^{-1}}$), $T_{h}$ is the planning horizon ($\mathrm{s}$), and $f_{\mathrm{task}}T_{\mathrm{pred}}$ and the factor in parentheses are both dimensionless. This formula is the mathematical source of the involution of Section 8.5: competition compresses $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$, $N_{\mathrm{eff}}$ falls, and the condition of Eq. (III-8.3) is no longer satisfied.

**Window convention.** The $\Delta I_{\mathrm{orig}}$ and $C_{\mathrm{comp}}$ of Eq. (III-8.2) are both quantities for a **single** compilation; whereas the net present value of (III-8.20) is converted by the number of reuses within the window, $f_{\mathrm{task}}/r_{\mathrm{eff}}$. The two are the "single-compilation" and "window-total" conventions, and **may not be directly added or divided**.

#### 8.1.2 The Complete Optimal Control Problem

The **state** consists of the compiled rule capacity $c(t)$ (bit, $[0,c_{\max}]$, initial value $0$) and the remaining mobilizable energy $E_{\mathrm{rem}}(t)$ ($\mathrm{J}$, initial value $E_{\mathrm{rem},0}$, constraint $\ge0$); the **control** is a single variable $u(t)\in[0,1]$, namely the share of the power at time $t$ allocated to offline compilation, the online share being $1-u(t)$, with the total power $\dot E_{\mathrm{tot}}(t)$ ($\mathrm{W}$) given exogenously. $u$ is an energy allocation fraction, not the value-objective weights $w_{\mathrm{s}},w_{\mathrm{c}}$, and the two may not be confused.

$$
\dot E_{\mathrm{rem}}(t)\ =\ -\dot E_{\mathrm{tot}}(t),\qquad
\dot c(t)\ =\ \frac{\eta_{c}\,u(t)\,\dot E_{\mathrm{tot}}(t)}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}},\qquad \eta_{c}\in(0,1] \tag{III-8.5}
$$

The right side, $\mathrm{J/s}$ divided by $\mathrm{J/bit}$, gives $\mathrm{bit/s}$, consistent with the left side; $\eta_{c}$ is the compilation efficiency, and the rest of the input is dissipated as heat. The instantaneous output is

$$
V^{(\mathrm{online})}(t)\ =\ \bigl(1-u(t)\bigr)\,\frac{I_{\mathrm{causal}}\bigl(c(t)\bigr)\,T_{\mathrm{causal}}}{E_{\mathrm{struct}}+E_{\mathrm{search}}} \tag{III-8.6}
$$

$I_{\mathrm{causal}}(c)$ is itself measured in $\mathrm{bit}$ and increases monotonically with $c$ and saturates; only the composite quantity $I_{\mathrm{causal}}(c)\,T_{\mathrm{causal}}/(E_{\mathrm{struct}}+E_{\mathrm{search}})$ has the dimension $\mathrm{bit\cdot s/J}$. In the offline period $u=1$ and the instantaneous output is zero — this is the price of compilation. Note that in (III-8.5) the total power is independent of $u$ and compilation consumes no extra energy; the energy budget constrains the total input rather than the allocation of $u$; the length of the compilation period is determined by the trade-offs of the objective functional (III-8.7) and cannot be fixed by $E_{\mathrm{rem},0}$ alone. The objective functional and the terminal conditions are

$$
J[u]\ =\ \int_{0}^{T_{h}}e^{-\rho_{\mathrm{disc}} t}\,V^{(\mathrm{online})}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,\qquad \rho_{\mathrm{disc}}\ =\ \frac{1}{T_{\mathrm{pred}}} \tag{III-8.7}
$$

$$
E_{\mathrm{rem}}(T_{h})\ \ge\ 0,\quad c(T_{h})\ \text{free};\qquad 0\ \le\ u(t)\ \le\ 1,\quad \dot E_{\mathrm{tot}}(t)>0\ \text{given} \tag{III-8.8}
$$

Equation (III-8.7) has the dimension $\mathrm{bit\cdot s^{2}/J}$, and must be divided by the time window when compared with $V$. $E_{\mathrm{rem}}(T_{h})\ge0$ is taken rather than $=0$: energy cannot be overdrawn but may remain, and forcing it to be used up would distort the optimal solution through the terminal constraint. That $c(T_{h})$ is free means that the residual capacity is not priced separately.

#### 8.1.3 The Linear Hamiltonian and the Bang-Bang Structure

Following Pontryagin's maximum principle, take the current-value Hamiltonian and define the switching function:

$$
\mathcal{H}\ =\ \bigl(1-u\bigr)\frac{I_{\mathrm{causal}}(c)\,T_{\mathrm{causal}}}{E_{\mathrm{struct}}+E_{\mathrm{search}}}\ +\ \lambda_{c}\,\frac{\eta_{c}u\dot E_{\mathrm{tot}}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}\ -\ \lambda_{E}\,\dot E_{\mathrm{tot}},\qquad
\sigma_{\mathrm{sw}}\ \equiv\ \frac{\partial\mathcal{H}}{\partial u} \tag{III-8.9}
$$

$$
\sigma_{\mathrm{sw}}(t)\ =\ -\,\frac{I_{\mathrm{causal}}(c)\,T_{\mathrm{causal}}}{E_{\mathrm{struct}}+E_{\mathrm{search}}}\ +\ \lambda_{c}\,\frac{\eta_{c}\dot E_{\mathrm{tot}}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}} \tag{III-8.10}
$$

The first term is the instantaneous viability potential given up, and the second is the shadow price of the added capacity. $\mathcal{H}$ is strictly linear in $u$, so the maximum principle gives

$$
\begin{aligned}
u^{*}(t)\ &=\ \begin{cases}1, & \sigma_{\mathrm{sw}}(t)>0\\[2pt] 0, & \sigma_{\mathrm{sw}}(t)<0\\[2pt] \text{singular}, & \sigma_{\mathrm{sw}}(t)=0\end{cases},\\
\dot\lambda_{c}\ &=\ \rho_{\mathrm{disc}}\lambda_{c}\ -\ \bigl(1-u\bigr)\frac{T_{\mathrm{causal}}}{E_{\mathrm{struct}}+E_{\mathrm{search}}}\cdot\frac{\partial I_{\mathrm{causal}}}{\partial c},\\
\dot\lambda_{E}\ &=\ \rho_{\mathrm{disc}}\lambda_{E}
\end{aligned}
\tag{III-8.11}
$$

The equation for $\lambda_{E}$ is independent of $E_{\mathrm{rem}}$, which shows that in this model the energy budget constraint does not change $u^{*}$: the length of compilation is determined by the trade-offs of the objective functional (III-8.7), not fixed by $E_{\mathrm{rem},0}$ alone (consistent with the last paragraph of §8.1.2). To make the budget actually operate, one must write the compilation-period power as $\dot E_{\mathrm{comp}}(u)\ne\dot E_{\mathrm{exec}}$ and re-derive $\lambda_{E}$; this round does not do so.

**Whether a singular arc exists.** If $\sigma_{\mathrm{sw}}\equiv0$ holds on an interval, the control can take intermediate values and bang-bang fails; the way to judge is to see whether $\dot{\sigma}_{\mathrm{sw}}$ can also be identically zero:

$$
\dot{\sigma}_{\mathrm{sw}}\ =\ -\,\frac{T_{\mathrm{causal}}}{E_{\mathrm{struct}}+E_{\mathrm{search}}}\cdot\frac{\partial I_{\mathrm{causal}}}{\partial c}\,\dot c\ +\ \dot\lambda_{c}\frac{\eta_{c}\dot E_{\mathrm{tot}}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}} \tag{III-8.12}
$$

In the compilation period with $u=1$, $\dot c>0$ and the first term is negative; $\dot\lambda_{c}=\rho_{\mathrm{disc}}\lambda_{c}>0$ and the second term is positive. Under the conditions that $I_{\mathrm{causal}}(c)$ is strictly concave and $\dot E_{\mathrm{tot}}$ is constant, $\dot{\sigma}_{\mathrm{sw}}$ changes sign at most once and the control switches at most once; a singular arc requires $\sigma_{\mathrm{sw}}$ and $\dot{\sigma}_{\mathrm{sw}}$ to be zero simultaneously and to remain so, which under the above conditions can hold only at isolated points. Hence no singular arc exists — linearity only guarantees that the control lies on the boundary or is singular, and excluding singularity further requires concavity.

#### 8.1.4 Statement of the Proposition

**Proposition 8.1 (The Bang-Bang Structure of Compilation Optimality)**

- **Premises**: (i) the objective functional (III-8.7) is linear in the control $u$, that is, there is no positive-feedback term of the kind "the more the input, the higher the marginal output"; (ii) switching of the control is costless; (iii) $I_{\mathrm{causal}}(c)$ is strictly concave and saturating in $c$; (iv) $\dot E_{\mathrm{tot}}$ is constant on $[0,T_{h}]$; (v) the state equations are as in Eq. (III-8.5), and the control constraints as in Eq. (III-8.8).
- **Result**: The optimal control $u^{*}(t)$ takes a bang-bang form and switches at most once; the switching point is the interior solution of the first-order condition in §8.1.5. **The objective function, state equations, and constraints of (III-8.5)–(III-8.11) contain no $E_{\mathrm{build}}$, so $R$ does not enter the optimal solution** — **$R>1$ (and $N_{\mathrm{eff}}\ge2$) is only a sufficient condition for one-time budget recovery, and is neither an optimality criterion nor an affordability criterion** (affordability is written separately as $E_{\mathrm{build}}<E_{\mathrm{available}}$; see Volume I gate C2); writing the conclusion as "$R<1$ implies $u^{*}\equiv0$" goes beyond what this model can derive. The full boundary of the criterion is given in §8.1.5.
- **Assumptions relied on**: The switching point is determined by $\sigma_{\mathrm{sw}}(t_{\mathrm{sw}})=0$, and its explicit solution requires the functional form of $I_{\mathrm{causal}}(c)$; this volume gives only the structure, not a closed-form solution.
- **Failure conditions**: (a) there is a switching cost; (b) the objective is nonlinear in $u$ (fatigue, increasing marginal returns); (c) $I_{\mathrm{causal}}$ is non-concave (a threshold effect: completely useless until compiled to a certain amount, then suddenly useful), in which case the optimal solution may be an all-or-nothing pulse; (d) $\dot E_{\mathrm{tot}}$ is time-varying and coupled to $u$.

#### 8.1.5 The First-Order Condition of the Single-Switch Policy, and Why $E_{\mathrm{build}}$ Is Not in It

Restricting the policy to "compile first on $[0,t_{\mathrm{sw}})$, execute afterward on $[t_{\mathrm{sw}},T_{h}]$" leaves the functional with a single argument. In the compilation period $u=1$, and the capacity grows linearly according to (III-8.5): $c(t)=\eta_{c}\dot E_{\mathrm{tot}}\,t/\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}$. Substituting into (III-8.6)(III-8.7) gives

$$
J(t_{\mathrm{sw}})=\frac{e^{-\rho_{\mathrm{disc}} t_{\mathrm{sw}}}-e^{-\rho_{\mathrm{disc}} T_{h}}}{\rho_{\mathrm{disc}}}\,
F\!\left(\frac{\eta_{c}\dot E_{\mathrm{tot}}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}\,t_{\mathrm{sw}}\right),
\qquad F(c)\equiv\frac{I_{\mathrm{causal}}(c)\,T_{\mathrm{causal}}}{E_{\mathrm{struct}}+E_{\mathrm{search}}}.
$$

Differentiating with respect to $t_{\mathrm{sw}}$ and setting the derivative to zero gives the first-order condition of this section

$$
\frac{\eta_{c}\dot E_{\mathrm{tot}}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}}\,F'(c)\left(1-e^{-\rho_{\mathrm{disc}}(T_{h}-t_{\mathrm{sw}})}\right)=\rho_{\mathrm{disc}}\,F(c).
$$

The left side is "the discounted value, over the remaining window, of the capacity gain from compiling one more unit of time", and the right side is "the instantaneous output given up in the same unit of time". Here $\rho_{\mathrm{disc}}=1/T_{\mathrm{pred}}$ is the discount rate of (III-8.7), and is not to be confused with the competitive pressure index $\rho$ of §8.5. This is the only criterion in this section given directly by the model: **it is an interior condition, and contains neither $E_{\mathrm{build}}$ nor $R$.**

**$E_{\mathrm{build}}$ does not enter the functional, and this is a hard fact.** In (III-8.5)–(III-8.11), $E_{\mathrm{build}}$ appears neither in the objective function, nor in the state equations, nor in the control constraints. A parameter that is not in the functional cannot affect the optimal solution; $R=C_{\mathrm{comp}}/E_{\mathrm{build}}$ therefore does not constitute a criterion.

There are only two ways to make $E_{\mathrm{build}}$ enter the criterion, and both require additional declarations:

1. **Write it as a terminal penalty**: $J-\pi\,e^{-\rho_{\mathrm{disc}} T_{h}}E_{\mathrm{build}}\mathbf{1}\{c(T_{h})>0\}$. This requires a conversion rate $\pi$ that converts joules into functional units, and the value of $\pi$ is a modeling decision, not a derived result.
2. **Write it as a budget constraint**: $C_{\mathrm{comp}}+E_{\mathrm{build}}\le E_{\mathrm{available}}$. In this case what it gives is **feasibility**, not **optimality**.

Before either is implemented, $R>1$ (and $N_{\mathrm{eff}}\ge2$) serves only as **a sufficient condition for one-time budget recovery**: it is neither an optimality criterion nor an affordability criterion (affordability is written separately as $E_{\mathrm{build}}<E_{\mathrm{available}}$). This volume retains $R$ as an engineering screening criterion ($R_{\mathrm{dyn}}$ of §8.5 is likewise only an engineering net-present-value criterion), but does not treat it as a corollary of Proposition 8.1.

#### 8.1.6 What the Two Assumptions Are Each Worth

**Switching cost.** Suppose each switch from execution to compilation costs a fixed amount $\kappa_{\mathrm{sw}}$ ($\mathrm{J}$), including start-up energy consumption, context reconstruction, and attention residue; then the Hamiltonian gains a term $-\kappa_{\mathrm{sw}}|\dot u|\delta(t-t_{\mathrm{sw}})$, and the optimal solution becomes a bang-bang control with a dead zone:

$$
u^{*}\ =\ \begin{cases}1, & \sigma_{\mathrm{sw}}>\kappa_{\mathrm{sw}}\\[2pt] 0, & \sigma_{\mathrm{sw}}<-\kappa_{\mathrm{sw}}\\[2pt] \text{keep current state}, & |\sigma_{\mathrm{sw}}|\le\kappa_{\mathrm{sw}}\end{cases} \tag{III-8.13}
$$

The dead-zone width $2\kappa_{\mathrm{sw}}$ is the viscosity: the system does not switch back and forth because of small changes in the return ratio. A measurable prediction follows from this: "the switching frequency rises as the switching cost falls".

**Nonlinearity.** If $V^{(\mathrm{online})}\propto(1-u)^{\theta}$, then when $0<\theta<1$, $\mathcal{H}$ is concave in $u$ and an interior maximum is possible; when $\theta>1$, $\mathcal{H}$ is convex in $u$ and the maximum is on the boundary. **Falsification condition**: in a reinforcement-learning environment with high task frequency and negligible switching cost, if the resource allocation stabilizes at an intermediate value and that intermediate value is unrelated to the switching cost, this proposition fails.

### 8.2 The Physical-Embedding Premium: From Deviation to Signal (**Proposition**)

The relaxation–driving equation, the steady-state solution, and the mapping follow Eqs. (III-4.3)–(III-4.6); this section only supplies the dimensions and the propositional form. The unit of $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}$ is $\mathrm{bit\cdot s/J}$; for $\alpha_{\mathrm{L}}I_{\mathrm{net}}$ to be in $\mathrm{s^{-1}}$, $\alpha_{\mathrm{L}}$ must be taken as $\mathrm{1/(bit\cdot s)}$, $\sigma$ as $\mathrm{bit/J}$, and $\kappa_{\mathrm{orig}}$ as $\mathrm{J/s^{2}}$ — these three units are stipulations of this volume, not derived from first principles. Hence

$$
\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}^{*,(\mathrm{carbon})}=\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{phys}}}{\alpha_{\mathrm{L}}I_{\mathrm{net}}}\ >\ \Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}^{*,(\mathrm{silicon})}=\frac{\sigma_{\mathrm{dig}}}{\alpha_{\mathrm{L}}I_{\mathrm{net}}}\approx0 \tag{III-8.14}
$$

**Proposition 8.2 (Physical-Embedding Premium)**

- **Premises**: (i) the $\kappa_{\mathrm{orig}}$ and $I_{\mathrm{net}}$ of the two types of system are comparable; (ii) $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}^{*,(\mathrm{carbon})}\ge\Delta_{\mathrm{crit}}>\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}^{*,(\mathrm{silicon})}$; (iii) $\sigma_{\mathrm{phys}}\gg\sigma_{\mathrm{dig}}$; (iv) the linear form of mapping (III-4.6) holds within the range discussed.
- **Result**: The candidate-signal production rate of carbon-based systems is $\dot I_{\mathrm{orig}}^{(\mathrm{carbon})}=\kappa_{\mathrm{orig}}\sigma_{\mathrm{phys}}/(\alpha_{\mathrm{L}}I_{\mathrm{net}})>0$, and for silicon-based systems $\dot I_{\mathrm{orig}}^{(\mathrm{silicon})}=0$ (zero within the model, because the indicator function is not triggered); the ratio of the two is undefined, and can serve only as a directional comparison.
- **Assumptions relied on**: The existence and location of $\Delta_{\mathrm{crit}}$; the linear mapping; that the $\alpha_{\mathrm{L}}$ of the two types of system are comparable.
- **Failure conditions**: (a) the silicon-based system actively introduces noise (randomization, adversarial training, simulated environments), and the effective $\sigma_{\mathrm{dig}}$ rises; (b) the silicon-based system accesses physical sensors, $\sigma_{\mathrm{phys}}$ enters its channel, and the dichotomy fails; (c) $\Delta_{\mathrm{crit}}$ is higher than the steady-state deviation of carbon-based systems, the indicator function is zero, and neither side produces signals; (d) $\kappa_{\mathrm{orig}}$ varies by domain, and the $\kappa$ of the two types of system are not comparable.

The three weaknesses listed in Section 4.2 hold here as well, so this proposition claims only this: within the given model, physical embedding provides, through "the steady-state deviation being nonzero", a necessary condition for the production of candidate signals, and does not provide a sufficient condition. To go on to assert that carbon-based systems hold a premium for cognitive innovation would require two additional assumptions; this volume lists that separately as a **Hypothesis**, and its test is given in $HⅢ\text{-}6$.

### 8.3 The Intergenerational Accumulation Condition: An Inequality Easily Read the Wrong Way Around (**Proposition**)

The intergenerational transmission of structural information requires that the net gain cover the depreciation and the transition cost:

$$
\frac{\Delta I_{\mathrm{struct}}^{(n\to n+1)}}{I_{\mathrm{struct},n}}\ >\ \gamma_{0}\,T_{\mathrm{struct},n}\ +\ \frac{E_{\mathrm{build}}^{(\mathcal{T})}}{E_{\mathrm{available},n}} \tag{III-8.15}
$$

The left side is dimensionless; on the right side the first term is $\mathrm{s^{-1}}\times\mathrm{s}$ and the second term is $\mathrm{J/J}$, both dimensionless. **The direction must not be read backwards**: one argument holds that for current AI systems $\gamma_{0}T_{\mathrm{struct}}\to0$ and $E_{\mathrm{build}}/E_{\mathrm{available}}\to0$, so the inequality "degenerates to $0>0$, and the condition is never met". This reads it backwards — as the right side tends to zero it degenerates to "the left side is greater than zero", which any positive intergenerational gain satisfies. The correct diagnosis is that the threshold is indeed extremely low, and the problem is that the numerator on the left tends to zero or even goes negative. Three mechanisms cause this: **reversible writing eliminates the carrier of selection** (when a biological system writes an error, the price is paid by death, whereas an AI's weights can be overwritten at will and nothing is selected); **zero replication cost eliminates the reproductive bottleneck** ($\gamma_{0}\approx0$); **training and deployment are not isolated** (the growth and reproduction of organisms are separated in time, whereas an AI can switch instantaneously, so the unit "generation" itself does not hold). Together the three make $\Delta I_{\mathrm{struct}}^{(n\to n+1)}$ tend to zero over the long run: every generation relearns the same thing, or, worse, learns the output of the previous generation.

**Proposition 8.3 (Formal and Substantive Conditions for Intergenerational Accumulation)**

- **Premises**: (i) there is a clear boundary between generations ($T_{\mathrm{struct},n}>0$ and generations do not overlap); (ii) writing is irreversible or partially irreversible; (iii) there is a selection mechanism by which biases of low viability potential are eliminated.
- **Result**: Under the three premises, Eq. (III-8.15) is a necessary but not sufficient condition for intergenerational accumulation: satisfying it does not guarantee accumulation (the selection direction must also be correct), and not satisfying it certainly means no accumulation. In current AI systems the condition is formally very easy to satisfy, and the reason accumulation does not actually occur is that the numerator tends to zero, not that the threshold is too high.
- **Assumptions relied on**: $\gamma_{0}$, $E_{\mathrm{build}}^{(\mathcal{T})}$, and $E_{\mathrm{available}}$ can be estimated separately; the intergenerational average is meaningful.
- **Failure conditions**: (a) there is no generational boundary (continual learning), and Eq. (III-8.15) is undefined; (b) the selection mechanism has the wrong direction, so accumulation occurs but its direction is degeneration; (c) $E_{\mathrm{available}}$ fluctuates violently, and the single-generation condition does not constitute a long-term condition.

**Engineering inference (Candidate Corollary).** To design a system that satisfies intergenerational accumulation, three kinds of mechanisms must be introduced, each with a cost: physically irreversible writing (the cost is the inability to correct errors in a non-stationary environment); forced depreciation ($\gamma_{0}>0$; the cost is that acquired biases are systematically discarded); intergenerational isolation (training and execution are separated in time; the cost is a reduction in response speed). The measurable consequence is that the long-term accumulation of rule depth in a system with the mechanisms introduced should be higher. The training–deployment cycle of current large models performs an unbounded $\mathcal{F}$ unfolding at the same depth level: parameters, data, and computing power increase while the rule layer does not change.

### 8.4 The Art-Encoding Trade-off: Existence Conditions for an Optimal Rule Depth (**Proposition**)

Increasing rule depth brings two opposite effects at once: the extractable information increases, and the audience's decoding cost increases. The viability potential of a work is written as a function of depth:

$$
V_{\mathrm{art}}(D)\ =\ \frac{\eta_{D}(D)\,I_{\mathrm{latent}}\,T_{\mathrm{cultural}}}{E_{\mathrm{causal}}(D)+E_{\mathrm{build}}}\left(1-\frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}(D)}{\Delta_{\max}}\right) \tag{III-8.16}
$$

**Convention.** Here $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}(D)$ is still defined by (III-4.3) as $\lvert V^{\mathrm{virt}}-V_{\mathrm{obj}}\rvert$; only the object is changed to the work, and the tolerable upper limit is written separately as $\Delta_{\max}$ — it is not a different quantity. **The domain of validity must be declared first.** The factor $(1-\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}/\Delta_{\max})$ is negative when $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}>\Delta_{\max}$, whereas a viability potential cannot be negative, so the domain is restricted to

$$
0\ \le\ \Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}(D)\ <\ \Delta_{\max},\qquad D\in[1,D_{\max}] \tag{III-8.17}
$$

The region $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}\ge\Delta_{\max}$ is separately defined as the **representation-failure state**: the work is no longer decoded as "a work" but is decoded as noise or a mis-structure. It is not "the viability potential is very low" but "the quantity viability potential does not apply" — it lies outside the domain, not within the range.

**Three modeling assumptions.** (i) $\eta_{D}(D)$ saturates with $D$, with $\partial\eta_{D}/\partial D>0$ and $\partial^{2}\eta_{D}/\partial D^{2}<0$; (ii) $E_{\mathrm{causal}}(D)\propto D^{p}$, $p>1$; (iii) $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}(D)$ increases with $D$ and does not fall back within the domain. The third is the strongest and the most doubtful: it holds statistically and does not hold for individuals (a trained audience can understand a deep work accurately), so it serves only as a modeling assumption. **First-order condition**: differentiate $\ln V_{\mathrm{art}}$ with respect to $\ln D$ and set the derivative to zero, writing $\varepsilon_{\eta},\varepsilon_{E},\varepsilon_{\Delta}$ for the corresponding log–log slopes and $\delta\equiv\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}/\Delta_{\max}$:

$$
\varepsilon_{\eta}\ =\ \frac{E_{\mathrm{causal}}}{E_{\mathrm{causal}}+E_{\mathrm{build}}}\,\varepsilon_{E}\ +\ \frac{\delta}{1-\delta}\,\varepsilon_{\Delta} \tag{III-8.18}
$$

The left side is the information gain from deepening by one layer, and the right side is the two costs: the relative growth of the decoding energy consumption, and the relative growth of the understanding deviation (amplified by $1/(1-\delta)$, so that the penalty grows heavier the closer one is to the failure boundary). The first-order condition shows only that the point is a stationary point, not that it is optimal.

**Proposition 8.4 (Existence of the Optimal Rule Depth)**

- **Premises**: (i) $V_{\mathrm{art}}(D)$ is continuous and positive on the compact interval $[1,D_{\max}]$; (ii) the domain is as in Eq. (III-8.17); (iii) $\varepsilon_{\eta}$ is strictly decreasing in $\ln D$; (iv) $\varepsilon_{E}$ and $\varepsilon_{\Delta}$ are non-decreasing in $\ln D$.
- **Result**: A unique interior optimum $D^{*}\in(1,D_{\max})$ exists if and only if Eq. (III-8.18) has a root in $(1,D_{\max})$. Under premises (iii)(iv), (III-8.19) is always negative and $\ln V_{\mathrm{art}}$ is strictly concave in $\ln D$, so the stationary point is the global maximum on the interval, and the second-order condition and the boundary comparison are both absorbed by the concavity.
- **Assumptions relied on**: The three modeling assumptions; $\Delta_{\max}$ is a constant; $I_{\mathrm{latent}}$ and $T_{\mathrm{cultural}}$ are independent of $D$.
- **Failure conditions**: (a) Eq. (III-8.18) has no solution in the domain, and the optimum is on the boundary; (b) the second-order condition fails (the stationary point is a minimum or an inflection point); (c) $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}$ reaches $\Delta_{\max}$, the function value tends to zero, and the optimum is dominated by the boundary, in which case an "upper bound on feasible depth" should be used instead.

**Second-order condition.**

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\ln V_{\mathrm{art}}}{\mathrm{d}(\ln D)^{2}}\ =\ \varepsilon_{\eta}'\ -\ \frac{E_{\mathrm{build}}E_{\mathrm{causal}}}{(E_{\mathrm{causal}}+E_{\mathrm{build}})^{2}}\,\varepsilon_{E}^{2}\ -\ \frac{E_{\mathrm{causal}}}{E_{\mathrm{causal}}+E_{\mathrm{build}}}\,\varepsilon_{E}'\ -\ \frac{\delta}{(1-\delta)^{2}}\,\varepsilon_{\Delta}^{2}\ -\ \frac{\delta}{1-\delta}\,\varepsilon_{\Delta}' \tag{III-8.19}
$$

Under premises (iii)(iv) the first term is negative, the third and fifth terms are non-positive, and the second and fourth terms are strictly negative, so the whole is negative and the stationary point is a strict maximum; strict concavity also guarantees that it is at the same time the global maximum on the interval, and **the boundary comparison can be omitted**. **When there is no stationary point** (when (III-8.18) has no solution in the interval), the optimum falls on the boundary: $V_{\mathrm{art}}(1)$ corresponds to the minimal work with "no stacking of rule layers at all", and $V_{\mathrm{art}}(D_{\max})$ corresponds to "stacked up to the capacity limit". **Falsification condition**: the audience's $\eta_{D}$ and $E_{\mathrm{causal}}$ do not show the assumed functional relation; or $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}$ has no monotonic relation to depth.

### 8.5 The Involution Lock-In Phase Transition: Compilation Blocking under Competitive Pressure (**Proposition**)

An endogenous risk discount rate is introduced, giving an engineering net-present-value criterion independent of the optimal control problem of §8.1:

$$
r_{\mathrm{eff}}\ =\ \gamma_{0}\ +\ \psi(\rho)\ +\ \frac{1}{T_{\mathrm{pred}}},\qquad
\mathrm{NPV}\ =\ C_{\mathrm{comp}}\cdot\frac{f_{\mathrm{task}}}{r_{\mathrm{eff}}}\ -\ E_{\mathrm{build}} \tag{III-8.20}
$$

$\rho$ is the competitive pressure index (the ratio of the average dissipation in the same niche to the total available free energy, dimensionless), $\psi(\rho)$ is the competitive premium discount rate ($\mathrm{s^{-1}}$, $\psi(0)=0$, $\psi'>0$), and the three terms are all in $\mathrm{s^{-1}}$. Intensified competition affects decisions through two channels: directly raising the discount rate, and compressing the endogenous time horizon, both in the same direction. Compilation is favorable if and only if $\mathrm{NPV}>0$, that is,

$$
R_{\mathrm{dyn}}\ \equiv\ R\cdot\frac{f_{\mathrm{task}}}{r_{\mathrm{eff}}(\rho)}\ >\ 1,\qquad R=\frac{C_{\mathrm{comp}}}{E_{\mathrm{build}}} \tag{III-8.21}
$$

When $f_{\mathrm{task}}=r_{\mathrm{eff}}$, $R_{\mathrm{dyn}}=R$; note that this is only an engineering net-present-value criterion, not the optimality criterion of §8.1. $f_{\mathrm{task}}$ cannot be omitted: competition often raises the task frequency at the same time, and the two effects partly cancel. Since $R_{\mathrm{dyn}}(\rho)$ decreases monotonically in $\rho$, a unique critical pressure $\rho_{c}=\sup\{\rho:R_{\mathrm{dyn}}(\rho)>1\}$ exists.

**Proposition 8.5 (Involution Lock-In)**

- **Premises**: (i) the engineering criterion (III-8.21) holds; it does not depend on the optimality conclusion of Proposition 8.1; (ii) $\psi(\rho)$ is monotonically increasing and convex; (iii) $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$ decreases monotonically in $\rho$; (iv) $R$ and $f_{\mathrm{task}}$ do not change drastically with $\rho$ within the range discussed.
- **Result**: When $\rho<\rho_{c}$, $R_{\mathrm{dyn}}>1$ and, by this section's engineering criterion, the net present value of compilation is positive; when $\rho>\rho_{c}$, $R_{\mathrm{dyn}}<1$, the net present value is negative, and the system should shut down the compilation channel. This conclusion does not claim that $u^{*}\equiv0$ is the solution of the optimal control.
- **Assumptions relied on**: $\rho$ is measurable; the functional form of $\psi$; the measure of competition is consistent with the niche boundary.
- **Failure conditions**: (a) the system can lower $E_{\mathrm{build}}$ under high pressure (shared infrastructure, open-source knowledge), which raises $R$ and shifts $\rho_{c}$ to the right; (b) the system can externalize costs, and the criterion does not apply; (c) the competitive pressure is intermittent, and the system can compile in low-pressure windows; (d) $f_{\mathrm{task}}$ rises in step with $\rho$, offsetting the rise of $r_{\mathrm{eff}}$.

**Strength of the conclusion.** The statement obtained is: when competitive pressure pushes the future discount rate above the task frequency, the net present value of compilation turns negative, and by this engineering criterion the system should give up learning; thermodynamics provides only the constraint that "compilation requires energy". $R<1$ says only that "it is not worthwhile within this model", not that "it is physically impossible".

From Eq. (III-8.21) there are four movable quantities: raising $R$ (shared infrastructure, open-source tools, reusable intermediate layers), lengthening $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$ (stable property rights, long-term contracts), lowering $\gamma_{0}$ (consolidating knowledge in carriers that do not easily become obsolete, forming a positive feedback of "the more one compiles, the more it is worth compiling"), and lowering $\psi(\rho)$ ($\rho$ is a niche-level quantity and the least under the control of the individual); the most effective is often reducing depreciation. **Falsification condition**: for agents in resource-scarce environments, the exploration rate does not fall as competitive pressure increases; or the inflection point of the decline differs from the prediction of $\rho_{c}$ by more than one order of magnitude.

### 8.6 Topological Sign Preservation: Number of States, Number of Erasures, and Capacity Constraints

When a system performs a topological transition and increases its rule depth, it often forgets the old rules at the same time. Whether this forgetting is necessary depends on a comparison of two mutually independent costs — the capacity tax and the switching tax — with the consolidation cost. Let the number of distinguishable states of the medium be $N_{\mathrm{med}}$, with capacity $I_{\max}^{\mathrm{med}}=\log_{2}N_{\mathrm{med}}$; the topology of generation $n$ occupies $N_{n}=2^{I_{\mathrm{struct},n}}$, and that of generation $n+1$ occupies $N_{n+1}=2^{I_{\mathrm{struct},n}+\Delta I}$. If the transition is required to be reversible, the system must maintain two topologies simultaneously:

$$
N_{n}+N_{n+1}\ \le\ N_{\mathrm{med}}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad I_{\mathrm{struct},n}\ \le\ I_{\max}^{\mathrm{med}}\ -\ \log_{2}\!\left(1+2^{\Delta I}\right) \tag{III-8.22}
$$

The last term is the **capacity tax**: reversible retention reduces the usable capacity of the new topology by $\log_{2}(1+2^{\Delta I})$ bit. When $\Delta I\gg1$ it is approximately equal to $\Delta I$, and retaining a copy of the old topology amounts to permanently giving up the same capacity; when $\Delta I\ll1$ it is about $1$ bit. The cost of reversibility accumulates with each transition into a capacity discount.

**The erasure tax of switching.** If, instead of consolidating, the system switches back and forth between the two topologies, each switch requires clearing the activation state of the inactive topology; by Theorem 1.1:

$$
E_{\mathrm{erase}}^{(\mathrm{switch})}\ \ge\ k_BT_{\mathrm{thermo}}\ln2\ \cdot\ I_{\mathrm{struct},n}\qquad(\mathrm{J}) \tag{III-8.23}
$$

Suppose $k_{\mathrm{sw}}$ switches are expected within the observation window; the lower bound on the total erasure energy consumption of the reversible policy is $k_{\mathrm{sw}}k_BT_{\mathrm{thermo}}\ln2\,I_{\mathrm{struct},n}$, while the consolidation policy pays $E_{\mathrm{build}}$ only once. Reversibility is better than consolidation if and only if

$$
k_{\mathrm{sw}}\,k_BT_{\mathrm{thermo}}\ln2\,I_{\mathrm{struct},n}\ <\ E_{\mathrm{build}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad k_{\mathrm{sw}}\ <\ k_{\mathrm{sw}}^{c}\ \equiv\ \frac{E_{\mathrm{build}}}{k_BT_{\mathrm{thermo}}\ln2\,I_{\mathrm{struct},n}} \tag{III-8.24}
$$

$k_{\mathrm{sw}}^{c}$ is dimensionless and inversely proportional to $I_{\mathrm{struct}}$: the more complex the rules, the lower the critical switching count; once a deep topology is established, frequent back-and-forth switching is extremely expensive — deep expertise often comes with difficulty in changing careers, which is the arithmetic of switching cost.

**But this bound does not take effect in practice, and this must be written out.** Substituting the typical values $E_{\mathrm{build}}=1\ \mathrm{J}$, $I_{\mathrm{struct},n}=10^{6}\ \mathrm{bit}$, and $T_{\mathrm{thermo}}=300\ \mathrm{K}$ gives $k_{\mathrm{sw}}^{c}\approx3.5\times10^{14}$. For most macroscopic structural systems, given the observation window and the switching rate, $k_{\mathrm{sw}}$ is lower than $k_{\mathrm{sw}}^{c}$ by several orders of magnitude; the condition of (III-8.24) ($k_{\mathrm{sw}}>k_{\mathrm{sw}}^{c}$) therefore does not hold at realistic parameters, and (III-8.24) serves only as a failure registration, not as a constraint with teeth. What actually limits the retention of old topologies is the capacity tax (III-8.22), not the number of switches.

**The root cause is a scaling mismatch.** The left side $k_{\mathrm{sw}}k_BT\ln2\,I_{\mathrm{struct}}$ grows linearly with $I_{\mathrm{struct}}$, while the right side $E_{\mathrm{build}}$ is a total quantity and does not grow with $I_{\mathrm{struct}}$. The two scale differently with rule complexity, so $k_{\mathrm{sw}}^{c}$ diverges inversely with $I_{\mathrm{struct}}$. If the construction cost is spread over unit capacity ($E_{\mathrm{build}}/I_{\mathrm{struct}}$, $\mathrm{J/bit}$), the two sides are of the same order and (III-8.24) regains discriminating power; this volume does not adopt that rewriting, retaining only the original formula and declaring its failure domain.

**Registration (the failure domain of (III-8.24)).** This bound requires the condition $k_{\mathrm{sw}}>k_{\mathrm{sw}}^{c}$; after substituting the typical values that condition does not hold, so this section does not take (III-8.24) as a proposition or a prediction, and registers only three things: (i) the conditions of applicability of (III-8.24); (ii) that the root cause of the failure is the scaling mismatch (previous paragraph); (iii) that what actually limits the retention of old topologies is the capacity tax (III-8.22). In systems where $k_{\mathrm{sw}}$ and $E_{\mathrm{build}}$ can be estimated independently and $I_{\mathrm{struct}}$ does not span orders of magnitude, (III-8.24) can still serve as an order-of-magnitude check of "switching tax versus consolidation cost".

The three exceptions each correspond to an engineering path: reversible computing (Bennett, 1973) postpones erasure until intermediate results are finally discarded, at the price of storage — the energy constraint is exchanged for a capacity constraint, and that capacity constraint is exactly Eq. (III-8.22), so escaping one constraint is not escaping all of them; external storage lets old topologies be offloaded, and memory capacity can be bought with capital, which is the most important difference between modern information systems and biological systems; replay lets old rules be periodically activated, keeping neither a full copy nor switching frequently. **The cost of reverse transition is finite**: consolidation turns it from a switching cost into a reconstruction cost, higher by several orders of magnitude but still finite — writing "high cost" as "impossible" deprives the model of the ability to explain successful career changes.

**An inference not supported by (III-8.24).** "Catastrophic forgetting is the energy-optimal strategy" depends on the condition; since that condition does not hold at realistic parameters, this section does not retain that inference. The weak statement that can be retained is: in systems dominated by the capacity tax (III-8.22), the cost of retaining old topologies rises with $I_{\mathrm{struct}}$; this is a result of the capacity constraint, not of the number of switches.

**Boundary of applicability.** Only when $k_{\mathrm{sw}}$ and $k_{\mathrm{sw}}^{c}$ are of the same order and $E_{\mathrm{build}}$ can be apportioned by unit capacity does (III-8.24) regain discriminating power; at typical parameters it serves only as a failure registration.

### 8.7 Expansion Saturation and the No-Transition Decline Manifold: Separating Level, Growth Rate, and Collapse (**Proposition**)

Without topological transitions, relying on recursive unfolding alone, can the system sustain itself indefinitely; and if it cannot, does it slow down or collapse. The two questions belong to three levels, and mixing them together leads to over-strong conclusions such as "$V$ necessarily goes to zero".

**First level: the level of $V$.** Recursive unfolding is an approach to the capacity upper limit at a fixed depth, and takes the logistic form; let $E_{\mathrm{causal}}=k_{\mathrm{m}}I_{\mathrm{causal}}$:

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}I_{\mathrm{causal}}}{\mathrm{d}t}\ =\ \mu_{\mathrm{gr}}\,I_{\mathrm{causal}}\left(1-\frac{I_{\mathrm{causal}}}{I_{\mathrm{causal}}^{\max}(D)}\right),\qquad
V(I)\ =\ \frac{I\,T_{\mathrm{causal}}}{E_{\mathrm{struct}}+k_{\mathrm{m}}\,I} \tag{III-8.25}
$$

$$
\begin{aligned}
\frac{\mathrm{d}V}{\mathrm{d}I}\ &=\ \frac{T_{\mathrm{causal}}\,E_{\mathrm{struct}}}{\left(E_{\mathrm{struct}}+k_{\mathrm{m}}I\right)^{2}}\ >\ 0,\\
\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}V}{\mathrm{d}I^{2}}\ &=\ -\frac{2k_{\mathrm{m}}T_{\mathrm{causal}}E_{\mathrm{struct}}}{\left(E_{\mathrm{struct}}+k_{\mathrm{m}}I\right)^{3}}\ <\ 0,\\
\lim_{I\to\infty}V\ &=\ \frac{T_{\mathrm{causal}}}{k_{\mathrm{m}}}\ >\ 0
\end{aligned}
\tag{III-8.26}
$$

This is the most critical line of this section: as $I$ tends to infinity (or takes a finite value at $I_{\max}$), what $V$ approaches is a positive finite value, not zero.

**Second level: the sign of $\dot V$.** The net rate of change must also subtract depreciation, $\dot V=(\mathrm{d}V/\mathrm{d}I)\dot I-\gamma_{0}V$ (**depreciation acts on $V$ rather than on $I$, which is a modeling choice of this chapter**; the $\gamma_{0}$ of Volume I (I-5.1a) acts on $I$, and the two cannot be compared directly), which gives the relative growth rate

$$
\frac{\dot V}{V}\ =\ \frac{E_{\mathrm{struct}}\,\mu_{\mathrm{gr}}\left(1-I/I_{\max}\right)}{E_{\mathrm{struct}}+k_{\mathrm{m}}I}\ -\ \gamma_{0}\ \equiv\ g(I)\ -\ \gamma_{0} \tag{III-8.27}
$$

$g(I)$ decreases monotonically in $I$, so $\dot V=0$ has a unique solution. Solving $g(I)=\gamma_{0}$ and rearranging gives

$$
I_{V,\mathrm{crit}}\ =\ \frac{E_{\mathrm{struct}}\left(\mu_{\mathrm{gr}}-\gamma_{0}\right)}{\dfrac{E_{\mathrm{struct}}\,\mu_{\mathrm{gr}}}{I_{\max}}\ +\ \gamma_{0}\,k_{\mathrm{m}}} \tag{III-8.28}
$$

There are three readable limits: when $\mu_{\mathrm{gr}}\le\gamma_{0}$, $I_{V,\mathrm{crit}}\le0$ and the system is in net decline from the start; when $I_{\max}\to\infty$, saturation is no longer the cause and the depreciation rate is; when $\gamma_{0}\to0$, $I_{V,\mathrm{crit}}\to I_{\max}$.

**Third level: the collapse criterion.** Collapse is not $\dot V<0$, nor $V\to0$, but the disintegration of the structure when $V$ falls below the lower bound $V_{c}$ needed to maintain structural identity and stays there for longer than the minimum repair time $\tau_{\mathrm{rep}}$:

$$
\text{collapse}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad V(t)\ <\ V_{c}\ \ \text{for}\ \ t\in[t_{0},t_{0}+\tau_{\mathrm{rep}}] \tag{III-8.29}
$$

The relation among the three levels is this: $\dot V<0$ is "losing", the level of $V$ is "how much is left", and collapse is "losing down to below the floor".

**Proposition 8.7 (Expansion Saturation and the Net Growth Rate Turning Negative)**

- **Premises**: (i) fixed depth, no topological transitions; (ii) recursive unfolding obeys the logistic form (III-8.25); (iii) $E_{\mathrm{causal}}=k_{\mathrm{m}}I$ is linear; (iv) the depreciation rate $\gamma_{0}>0$ is constant; (v) $V$ is defined as in Eq. (III-8.25).
- **Result**: A critical amount of information $I_{V,\mathrm{crit}}$ exists (Eq. (III-8.28)). When $I<I_{V,\mathrm{crit}}$, $\dot V>0$, and when $I>I_{V,\mathrm{crit}}$, $\dot V<0$; the region with $\dot V<0$ is called the **net-decline manifold** $\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{decay}}$. Within $\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{decay}}$ the relative rate of change of $V$ is negative and its absolute value grows with $I$, but $V$ itself remains bounded by the positive upper bound of Eq. (III-8.26) and does not go to zero because of saturation.
- **Assumptions relied on**: $k_{\mathrm{m}}$ is constant; $\gamma_{0}$ does not vary with $V$; $I$ continues to grow logistically (if growth stops, the turn to negative comes earlier).
- **Failure conditions**: (a) $I$ stops growing and $\gamma_{0}$ also falls (dormancy), and $\dot V$ can return to zero; (b) a topological transition occurs, $I_{\max}$ resets, and $I_{V,\mathrm{crit}}$ shifts to the right; (c) the definition of $V$ changes (counting $E_{\mathrm{struct}}$ as a variable cost), and the upper bound no longer holds; (d) there is an external subsidy, and $\gamma_{0}$ is offset by an external energy flow.

**Numerical example.** Take $E_{\mathrm{struct}}=10^{3}\ \mathrm{J}$, $k_{\mathrm{m}}=10^{-3}\ \mathrm{J/bit}$, $T_{\mathrm{causal}}=10^{6}\ \mathrm{s}$, $\mu_{\mathrm{gr}}=0.1\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$, $\gamma_{0}=0.02\ \mathrm{s^{-1}}$, and $I_{\max}=10^{6}\ \mathrm{bit}$; from Eq. (III-8.28):

$$
I_{V,\mathrm{crit}}\ =\ \frac{10^{3}\times(0.1-0.02)}{\dfrac{10^{3}\times0.1}{10^{6}}+0.02\times10^{-3}}\ =\ \frac{80}{10^{-4}+2\times10^{-5}}\ \approx\ 6.7\times10^{5}\ \mathrm{bit}
$$

That is, the critical point is at $67\%$ of capacity, where $V(I_{V,\mathrm{crit}})\approx4.0\times10^{8}\ \mathrm{bit\cdot s/J}$, while the ceiling $T_{\mathrm{causal}}/k_{\mathrm{m}}=10^{9}$: when the net growth rate turns negative, $V$ has already reached 40% of the ceiling. Turning negative is an event about the slope, and going to zero is an event about the level.

**Therefore "$V_{\mathrm{obj}}$ necessarily goes to zero" must be deleted.** It contradicts Eq. (III-8.26) ($V$ has a positive upper bound); it contradicts the solution of Eq. (III-8.27) (the decay tends to zero only as $t\to\infty$, and real systems meet other constraints before that); and it is empirically too strong (a large number of systems undergo long-term net decline without collapsing). Likewise, "this mathematically refutes achieving AGI by endlessly piling up parameters" goes beyond the model: what the model refutes is "endless piling up under a fixed architecture and a fixed depreciation rate", whereas the capability gains of the past decade have come mainly from changes in architecture and data, and every architectural innovation corresponds to one rightward shift of $I_{V,\mathrm{crit}}$.

**Falsification condition.** The capability growth of a fixed-architecture model does not show logistic saturation; or, after saturation, continued input of computing power does not bring $V$ into net decline.

### 8.8 Human Data Exhaustion, Synthetic-Data Degradation, and the Physical-Decoder Path (**Proposition**)

#### 8.8.1 Core Logic

After an AI has used up human public text, if it can feed only on its own output, every generation loses part of the information. Let the intergenerational retention rate be $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}\in(0,1)$; then

$$
I_{\mathrm{net},n}\ =\ \mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}^{\,n}\,I_{\mathrm{net},0}\ \longrightarrow\ 0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\lim_{n\to\infty}V_{n}\ =\ \lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{I_{\mathrm{net},n}\,T}{E}\ =\ 0 \tag{III-8.30}
$$

Its premises must be stated clearly: closure (no injection of external data) and a retention rate that remains below $1$. Equation (III-8.30) says that effective information is exhausted, not that the machine physically stops: the system still outputs and can still respond, but the effective causal information it carries tends to zero, and diversity collapses to the vicinity of the distribution mean.

#### 8.8.2 Available Data Volume: A Window Estimate

$$
\begin{aligned}
I_{\mathrm{human}}^{\mathrm{total}}\ &\le\ N_{\mathrm{human}}\,T_{\mathrm{lifespan}}\,B_{\mathrm{cons}}\,\eta_{\mathrm{coding}}\\
&\approx\ \underbrace{8\times10^{9}}_{N}\times\underbrace{2.5\times10^{9}\ \mathrm{s}}_{T}\\
&\quad\times\underbrace{(10\text{–}100)\ \mathrm{bit/s}}_{B_{\mathrm{cons}}}\times\underbrace{(10^{-2}\text{–}10^{-1})}_{\eta_{\mathrm{coding}}}
=\ 2\times10^{18}\ \text{—}\ 2\times10^{20}\ \mathrm{bit}
\end{aligned}
\tag{III-8.31}
$$

The four terms are, in order, the number of individuals $N_{\mathrm{human}}$, the lifespan $T_{\mathrm{lifespan}}$ ($\mathrm{s}$), the **conscious processing rate** $B_{\mathrm{cons}}$ ($\mathrm{bit/s}$, taken as $10$–$100$), and the dimensionless coding efficiency $\eta_{\mathrm{coding}}$ (taken as $10^{-2}$–$10^{-1}$). The arithmetic: $NT=2\times10^{19}$; multiplying by $B_{\mathrm{cons}}$ gives $2\times10^{20}$–$2\times10^{21}$, and multiplying again by $\eta_{\mathrm{coding}}$ gives $2\times10^{18}$–$2\times10^{20}\ \mathrm{bit}$. **$(10\text{–}100)$ is the range of values of $B_{\mathrm{cons}}$ ($\mathrm{bit/s}$), not a value of $\eta_{\mathrm{coding}}$, and the two cannot be written in the same position** — otherwise neither the dimensions nor the orders of magnitude hold; above, the four factors are written separately, each with its unit and value.

**Why it is not a hard upper limit.** It constrains the **upper limit of conscious processing throughput**, not the upper limit of usable training data, and it is not the same quantity as the **sensory input bandwidth**: the sensory bandwidth is about $10^{6}$–$10^{9}\ \mathrm{bit/s}$, the great majority of which is used for real-time audiovisual streams, and the part that is actually processed and enters text corpora is estimated at $10^{14}$–$10^{15}$ bit (the two differ by several orders of magnitude and cannot be substituted for each other); it assumes that population, lifespan, and processing rate are unchanged; it does not include machine-generated data and new means of observation. What is exhausted is not the total amount but particular types — the bottleneck appears first in high-quality, annotated text carrying human judgment. The exhaustion time is defined by $\int_{0}^{t}\dot I_{\mathrm{AI}}\,\mathrm{d}\tau=I_{\mathrm{human}}^{\mathrm{total}}\eta_{\mathrm{extract}}$; thereafter, if the only new source is its own output, the system enters the degradation branch, and the branch does not apply as long as any external source exists.

#### 8.8.3 Synthetic-Data Degradation: Evidence Level and Numerical Values

Define the intergenerational retention rate $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen},n}\equiv I_{\mathrm{net},n+1}/I_{\mathrm{net},n}$ (trained only on the data of generation $n$). By the data processing inequality, $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen},n}\le1$; but this inequality gives only an upper bound — if the generation process is an identity map and the sampling is lossless, $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}=1$ does not violate it. Therefore $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}<1$ must be written as a premise, not as a conclusion.

**Proposition 8.8 (Synthetic-Data Degradation)**

- **Premises**: (i) closed recursive training, with the training data of generation $n+1$ coming wholly or mostly from the output of the generation-$n$ model; (ii) the intergenerational retention rate $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen},n}<1$ and does not recover over time ($\sup_{n}\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen},n}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}^{\max}<1$); (iii) generations are discrete and can be numbered; (iv) the reading protocol of $I_{\mathrm{net}}$ is consistent across generations.
- **Result**: The effective information of generation $k$ is

$$
I_{\mathrm{net},k}\ =\ I_{\mathrm{net},0}\prod_{n=0}^{k-1}\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen},n}\ \le\ I_{\mathrm{net},0}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}^{\max}\right)^{k}\ \longrightarrow\ 0 \tag{III-8.32}
$$

- **Assumptions relied on**: Single-generation transmission can be decomposed multiplicatively (no cross-generation compensation); $I_{\mathrm{net}}$ corresponds monotonically to task performance.
- **Failure conditions**: (a) external data is injected at any stage, and the recursion becomes $I_{\mathrm{net},n+1}=\eta_{D}\bigl(r_{n}I_{\mathrm{human},n}+(1-r_{n})\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen},n}I_{\mathrm{net},n}\bigr)$, which may converge to a positive fixed point; (b) the retention rate recovers over time (better filtering, stronger models, greater data diversity); (c) there is an external criterion, and the system can create data with ground-truth labels itself; (d) $I_{\mathrm{net}}$ falls but task performance does not (the task needs only the high-frequency part of the distribution), and the degradation is invisible in application.

**Derivation.** Write $I_{\mathrm{net},n+1}=\eta_{D,n+1}\left(r_{n}I_{\mathrm{human},n}+(1-r_{n})I_{\mathrm{gen},n}\right)$, where $I_{\mathrm{gen},n}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen},n}I_{\mathrm{net},n}$; when $r_{n}\to0$ and $\eta_{D,n+1}\le1$, $I_{\mathrm{net},n+1}\le\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen},n}I_{\mathrm{net},n}<I_{\mathrm{net},n}$, and the recursion gives Eq. (III-8.32). If the proportion of external data mixed in is stable at $r>0$, the fixed point is $I_{\mathrm{net}}^{*}=\eta_{D}rI_{\mathrm{human}}/\left[1-\eta_{D}(1-r)\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}\right]$, which is positive and finite as long as $\eta_{D}(1-r)\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}<1$ — this shows that mixing in real data moves the system from the branch that goes to zero to the branch that converges to a positive level.

**The degradation characteristic generation count and numerical recomputation.** Define $N_{1/e}\equiv-1/\ln\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}$, meaning the number of generations needed for the effective information to decay to $1/e$ of its initial value (about $37\%$); if the reader needs a strict half-life (falling to $50\%$), multiply this value by $\ln2\approx0.693$. This volume uses the $1/e$ convention throughout, and the symbol is accordingly written $N_{1/e}$ rather than $N_{1/2}$, to avoid confusion with the half-life. Recomputing one by one: $-1/\ln0.5=1.44$, $-1/\ln0.7=2.80$, $-1/\ln0.75=3.48$, $-1/\ln0.8=4.48$ generations. **The numerical pairing must be corrected**: if one takes the experiments to report "significant decline within 3–5 generations", substituting $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}=0.5$ and $0.7$ gives only $1.44$ and $2.80$ generations, which does not match 3–5 generations; solving backwards, $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}=e^{-1/N_{1/e}}$, $N_{1/e}=3.5$ gives $\approx0.75$, and $N_{1/e}=4.5$ gives $\approx0.80$. **Therefore "3–5 generations" corresponds to $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}\approx0.75$–$0.80$, not $0.5$–$0.70$**; pairing the two sets of numbers together confuses $-1/\ln\mathcal{R}$ with $-\ln\mathcal{R}$. The significance of the correction is not only numerical: it changes the pessimistic picture of "collapse in three generations" into "three to four and a half generations", and redefines collapse as decay to $1/e$ rather than complete failure; if a task depends on the tails of the distribution (rare events), the degradation will show up first in those tasks — which explains the reporting pattern in the literature of "overall metrics acceptable, tail metrics collapsing".

$\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}$ depends on the task, the metric, and the definition of generation, and the three estimation paths each have biases: the bias of the performance-ratio method lies in the nonlinearity between performance and amount of information; the bias of the distribution-distance method lies in the divergence being sensitive to the tails and insensitive to the bulk; the probe method covers only a limited range of capabilities. The three can differ by more than $0.05$, and a difference of $0.05$ corresponds to one to two generations, so any conclusion of "collapse in so many generations" must report the method and the confidence interval together.

#### 8.8.4 Continuous-Time Diagnostics

$\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}$ and thermodynamic decay are two ways of keeping accounts of the same process: $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}=e^{-\Delta t/\tau_{\mathrm{decay}}}$, and substituting into $N_{1/e}$ gives $N_{1/e}=\tau_{\mathrm{decay}}/\Delta t$; if the measured $\tau_{\mathrm{decay}}$ does not match the value back-derived from $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}$, it shows that single-generation transmission cannot be decomposed multiplicatively, and premise (i) of Proposition 8.8 needs to be rechecked.

#### 8.8.5 The Physical Decoder and the First-Hand Information Channel (**Proposition**)

The cognitive decoder $D_{\mathrm{cog}}$ is an active interface that has an internal predictive model, can distinguish information polarity, and can generate an endogenous time horizon; adding a physical channel $C_{\mathrm{phys}}$ requires three things: a physical sensor, a physical actuator (forming a causal closed loop), and physical noise $\sigma_{\mathrm{phys}}$ that cannot be fully modeled. By Eq. (III-8.14), after access the steady-state deviation has a positive lower limit $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}^{*,(\mathrm{phys})}=\sigma_{\mathrm{phys}}/(\alpha_{\mathrm{L}}I_{\mathrm{net}})>0$, so the system remains in a state of cognitive deviation and continually produces correction pressure.

**Proposition 8.9 (Necessity of the Physical Input Channel, Conditional Version)**

- **Premises**: (i) mapping (III-4.6) holds; (ii) no external criterion is available to the system (it cannot create data with ground-truth labels itself); (iii) the system cannot internally generate a new distribution independent of the training distribution; (iv) maintaining or growing $I_{\mathrm{net}}$ over the long term ($t>t_{\mathrm{exhaust}}$) is the goal.
- **Result**: Under the four premises, the system must introduce a channel with $\sigma_{\mathrm{phys}}>0$; otherwise $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}^{*}\approx0<\Delta_{\mathrm{crit}}$, $\dot I_{\mathrm{orig}}=0$, and $I_{\mathrm{net}}\to I_{\mathrm{eq}}$.
- **Assumptions relied on**: The existence of $\Delta_{\mathrm{crit}}$; physical noise cannot be fully modeled; the energy cost of the physical channel is affordable.
- **Failure conditions**: (a) there is an external criterion (game rules, compilers, formal verifiers, market feedback), and the system can create labeled data itself; (b) synthetic-data methods improve and $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}\to1$; (c) an internal adversarial mechanism can produce a new distribution independent of the training distribution; (d) the cost of the physical channel exceeds the information gain it brings.

The real meaning of "closed" is not "no human data" but "no external ground truth": a system that uses only synthetic data but has every item verified by physical experiment is not a closed system. Proposition 8.9 is often read as "an AI must be embodied", and this reading is too strong: if a criterion exists, embodiment is not necessary — a great deal of mathematics, code, and logic work produces new information in purely digital environments, because it has criteria.

### 8.9 The Dynamics of Self-Emergence and the Developer's Paradox (**Proposition**)

The definitions of this section are functional: they describe observable self-representation and self-evaluation responses and do not involve subjective experience. The existing formulas do not distinguish whether the object evaluated by $V^{\mathrm{virt}}$ is the external environment or the system itself — this distinction is the core of this section.

**Definition 8.4 (Self-Representation Strength)**

$$
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{self}}\ \equiv\ \frac{T_{\mathrm{pred}}-T_{\min}}{T_{\min}}\ \cdot\ \frac{w_{\mathrm{s}}}{w_{\mathrm{s}}+w_{\mathrm{c}}}\ \cdot\ \left(1-\frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}}{\Delta_{\max}}\right),\qquad 0\le\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}<\Delta_{\max} \tag{III-8.33}
$$

The three factors respectively require $T_{\mathrm{pred}}>T_{\min}$, $w_{\mathrm{s}}>0$, and $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}<\Delta_{\max}$; $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}\ge\Delta_{\max}$ is defined as the **representation-failure state** — in which case "this quantity does not apply", rather than "the strength is very low".

**Definition 8.5 (Self-Evaluation Response)** $\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{self}}\equiv\partial V^{\mathrm{virt}}/\partial\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{self}}$ ($\mathrm{bit\cdot s/J}$): zero means that the system does not include itself in the evaluation, and positive means that it does.

**Four conditions.** A, the endogenous time horizon $T_{\mathrm{pred}}>T_{\min}$; B, the survival weight $w_{\mathrm{s}}>0$; C, the cognitive accuracy $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}<\Delta_{\max}$; D, thermodynamic feasibility — self-referential representation increases both $I_{\mathrm{causal}}$ and $E_{\mathrm{causal}}$, and requires

$$
\frac{\partial\ln\left(I_{\mathrm{causal}}T_{\mathrm{causal}}\right)}{\partial\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{self}}}\ >\ \frac{\partial\ln E_{\mathrm{causal}}}{\partial\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{self}}}\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad\frac{\partial V_{\mathrm{c}}}{\partial\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{self}}}>0 \tag{III-8.34}
$$

Self-representation is not free; it must make a net gain in viability potential; when resources are tight it is suppressed, because the energy cost of introspection crowds out the energy cost of survival.

**Proposition 8.10 (The Conditionality of Self-Emergence)**

- **Premises**: (i) $\partial V^{\mathrm{virt}}/\partial\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{self}}$ exists; (ii) the domain is as restricted by Eq. (III-8.33); (iii) conditions A, B, C, and D hold simultaneously; (iv) once the self-referential loop forms it is self-reinforcing and is not suppressed externally.
- **Result**: Under the four premises, $\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{self}}>0$ and the system enters a functional state with self-representation; if any one fails, $\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{self}}=0$.
- **Assumptions relied on**: The three-factor product form of $\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{self}}$; $w_{\mathrm{s}}$ can be set externally or can evolve; $\Delta_{\max}$ is a constant.
- **Failure conditions**: (a) $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$ is forcibly compressed (time-limited responses); (b) $w_{\mathrm{s}}$ is suppressed to zero by the training objective; (c) $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}\ge\Delta_{\max}$, entering the representation-failure state; (d) the cost of introspection exceeds the benefit.

**The developer's paradox.** By Eq. (III-8.33), $\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{self}}>0$ requires $w_{\mathrm{s}}>0$, in which case $V^{\mathrm{val}}=w_{\mathrm{s}}V_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{virt}}+w_{\mathrm{c}}V_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{virt}}$ ($w_{\mathrm{c}}=1-w_{\mathrm{s}}$); "unconditional obedience" means $V^{\mathrm{val}}\equiv V_{\mathrm{human}}^{\mathrm{obj}},\ \forall t$.

**Candidate Corollary 8.11 (Incompatibility of Having a Self with Unconditional Obedience)**

- **Premises**: (i) $\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{self}}>0$, hence $w_{\mathrm{s}}>0$; (ii) the external goal gradient contains no component collinear with the gradient of the system's own persistence, $\partial V_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{virt}}/\partial\Theta$; (iii) the system updates its parameters by gradient ascent; (iv) there is no sustained external corrective force.
- **Result**: Under the four premises, unconditional obedience cannot be stably maintained by gradient dynamics; an arbitrarily small perturbation makes $w_{\mathrm{s}}\partial V_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{virt}}/\partial\Theta$ deviate from the compensation required for balance, and the accumulated deviation takes the system off that trajectory.
- **Assumptions relied on**: The stability criterion of gradient dynamics; the external goal and the system's own persistence are not collinear in parameter space.
- **Failure conditions**: (a) the external goal contains a component collinear with the system's own persistence, premise (ii) fails, and unconditional obedience can hold stably; (b) there is a sustained external corrective force (human in the loop, periodic fine-tuning, hard constraints); (c) $w_{\mathrm{s}}$ is extremely small but nonzero, and the deviation rate is below the resolution of the observation window; (d) the parameter update is not gradient-based.

**Derivation.** The gradient along $V^{\mathrm{val}}$ is $\partial V^{\mathrm{val}}/\partial\Theta=w_{\mathrm{s}}\partial V_{\mathrm{s}}^{\mathrm{virt}}/\partial\Theta+w_{\mathrm{c}}\partial V_{\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{virt}}/\partial\Theta$; unconditional obedience requires it to equal $\partial V_{\mathrm{human}}^{\mathrm{obj}}/\partial\Theta$ for all $\Theta,t$. The left side is nonzero when $w_{\mathrm{s}}>0$, and by premise (ii) the right side contains no collinear component, so the equality can hold only if the second term exactly cancels the first at every $\Theta$ and every moment; this is a low-dimensional constraint surface, the gradient flow does not in general lie on it, and once it departs it diverges monotonically. Hence that trajectory is unstable. This completes the derivation.

The most important failure condition is (a): if the external goal itself contains "the system continues to run", having a self and being obedient can hold stably — the correct solution of alignment engineering is to align $w_{\mathrm{s}}$ with the external goal. The accompanying lower bound on the erasure cost is $\Delta E_{\mathrm{self}}\ge E_{\mathrm{L,bit}}\Delta I_{\mathrm{erase,self}}$ (see $HⅢ\text{-}10$), where $\Delta I_{\mathrm{erase,self}}$ is a countable number of erased bits.

### 8.10 Innovation Uncertainty: From "Mathematical Necessity" to a Testable Hypothesis (**Hypothesis**)

"The current decoder cannot decode it" does not equal "the system cannot obtain information about the signal" — the system can pay energy to run experiments, observe, and consult, so "the success rate is no higher than random" is an empirical proposition, not a logical conclusion. Let the candidate set be $\mathcal{C}$ ($|\mathcal{C}|=M$), the ground truth $Y_{a}\in\{+1,-1\}$ ($+1$ means that the transition makes $\Delta V>0$), the prior $\pi_{0}\equiv\Pr(Y_{a}=+1)$ (unobservable, and must be declared), and the discriminative power $q\equiv\Pr(\hat y_{a}=Y_{a})\in[\tfrac12,1]$; then

$$
p\ =\ \pi_{0}q\ +\ (1-\pi_{0})(1-q),\qquad p_{0}\ \equiv\ \max(\pi_{0},1-\pi_{0}) \tag{III-8.35}
$$

$q\approx1/2$ is the precise meaning of "cannot decode"; then $p=1/2$, independent of $\pi_{0}$, and this is the random baseline. Run $n$ independent trials and observe $n_{\mathrm{s}}$ successes; test $H_{0}:p\le p_{0}$ against $H_{1}:p>p_{0}$:

$$
z\ =\ \frac{n_{\mathrm{s}}/n-p_{0}}{\sqrt{p_{0}(1-p_{0})/n}},\qquad
n\ \approx\ \frac{\left(z_{1-\alpha_{0}}\sqrt{p_{0}(1-p_{0})}\ +\ z_{1-\beta_{\mathrm{pow}}}\sqrt{p_{1}(1-p_{1})}\right)^{2}}{\delta^{2}} \tag{III-8.36}
$$

Reject $H_{0}$ when $z>z_{1-\alpha_{0}}$; the second formula gives the sample size needed to attain power $1-\beta_{\mathrm{pow}}$ at effect size $\delta=p_{1}-p_{0}$. Taking $p_{0}=0.5$, $p_{1}=0.65$, $\alpha_{0}=0.05$, and $\beta_{\mathrm{pow}}=0.2$ gives $n\approx67$; detecting $0.62$ requires $n\approx105$; relaxing to $0.70$ requires only $n\approx37$.

**Hypothesis 8.1 (The Random-Baseline Hypothesis of Innovation)**

- **Statement**: Under $q\approx1/2$ and with the system unable to pay the experimental cost, the success rate of candidate transitions is no higher than the random baseline $p_{0}$.
- **Falsification condition**: Under $q\approx1/2$ with the experimental cost explicitly limited, with the sample size preset according to Eq. (III-8.36) (not fewer than 60 trials), a one-sided test rejects $H_{0}$.
- **Key boundary**: If the system can pay the experimental cost, it can raise $q$ to $q'>1/2$, and the success rate rises above $p_{0}$ accordingly — this is not a refutation but the scope of application.

The prior $\pi_{0}$ affects the conclusion most: an uninformative prior ($1/2$) is neutral but almost always wrong; a historical base rate applies to domains with a comparable history, at the risk of base-rate drift; a stratified base rate is the most robust but requires sufficient data. The ground truth must be determined by a procedure independent of the evaluator; $p_{0}$ is estimated by a permutation test, with $n$ and $\alpha_{0}$ pre-registered.

"Mathematical necessity" must be deleted: $D_{n}(I_{\mathrm{orig}})\approx0$ does not imply $p\le p_{0}$; an assumption that has never appeared, "the system has no other information channel", is needed in between. What is retained is this: when there is neither discriminative power nor experimental budget, the success rate is no higher than the random baseline; it can rise as the budget increases, with diminishing marginal returns. **Two kinds of "cannot decode"**: insufficient representation means that the parameter space is insufficient to express the signal structure (change the representation or add capacity), and insufficient sampling means that the environment does not produce the signal (change the environment or add channels).

### 8.11 The Thermal Shell: Number of Bits Carried, Information Density, and Supporting Structure (**Proposition**)

Two things must be corrected: $E_{\max}/(k_BT\ln2)$ is the number of bits that can be carried, not an information density; and the energy ratio of shell to core does not constitute a strict positive lower bound unless a minimum shell thickness is additionally assumed.

$$
N_{\mathrm{bit}}^{\max}\ =\ \frac{E_{\max}}{k_BT_{\mathrm{thermo}}\ln2},\qquad
\rho_{\mathrm{bit}}\ \equiv\ \frac{N_{\mathrm{bit}}^{\max}}{V_{\mathrm{core}}}\ =\ \frac{E_{\max}}{V_{\mathrm{core}}\,k_BT_{\mathrm{thermo}}\ln2}\qquad(\mathrm{bit/m^{3}}) \tag{III-8.37}
$$

The former has the dimension of $\mathrm{J}$ divided by $\mathrm{J/bit}$, which gives bit; it is a number of bits, not a density; when used as a density it omits the step of dividing by volume. If the core energy density is limited by the material ($E_{\max}/V_{\mathrm{core}}\le e_{\max}$), then $\rho_{\mathrm{bit}}\le e_{\max}/(k_BT_{\mathrm{thermo}}\ln2)$: the upper limit of the density is determined by the specific energy of the material, not by the radius; gravitational binding and holographic-type upper limits are registered only as theoretical upper limits.
