**Heat-dissipation requirement and shell ratio.** Suppose the core power $\dot E_{\mathrm{core}}$ is discharged mainly by conduction through the shell; from $\dot E_{\mathrm{cool}}=\kappa A\Delta T/L\ge\dot E_{\mathrm{core}}$ and a spherical core with $A=4\pi R^{2}$:

$$
R\ \ge\ \sqrt{\frac{\dot E_{\mathrm{core}}\,L}{4\pi\kappa\,\Delta T}},\qquad
\Sigma\ \equiv\ \frac{E_{\mathrm{shell}}}{E_{\mathrm{core}}}\ \ge\ \frac{4\pi\left(\dfrac{\dot E_{\mathrm{core}}L}{4\pi\kappa\Delta T}\right)L\rho_{s}e_{s}}{\dot E_{\mathrm{core}}\,\tau_{\mathrm{op}}}\ =\ \frac{L^{2}\rho_{s}e_{s}}{\kappa\,\Delta T\,\tau_{\mathrm{op}}} \tag{III-8.38}
$$

**Convention**: the $\Sigma$ here is the **"embedded energy to operating energy"** ratio — $E_{\mathrm{shell}}$ is the energy stored in the shell material and $E_{\mathrm{core}}$ is the operating energy consumption; it is **not a mass ratio**, and it also differs from the "support plus heat-dissipation energy ratio" of Volume Z (Z-5.13); the three conventions have the same dimension but different meanings, and cannot be compared across places (see the convention note in Volume Z §5.6).

Here $E_{\mathrm{shell}}=4\pi R^{2}L\rho_{s}e_{s}$, $\rho_{s}$ is the shell density, $e_{s}$ is the specific energy ($\mathrm{J/kg}$), and $\tau_{\mathrm{op}}$ is the operating window; the numerator is $\mathrm{m^{2}}\cdot\mathrm{kg\,m^{-3}}\cdot\mathrm{J\,kg^{-1}}=\mathrm{J/m}$, and the denominator is $\mathrm{W\,m^{-1}K^{-1}}\cdot\mathrm{K}\cdot\mathrm{s}=\mathrm{J/m}$, so the ratio is dimensionless. This result depends on $L$, and $L$ has not been determined: as $L\to0$, $\Sigma\to0$ and the lower bound disappears. A positive lower bound is obtained only if the shell thickness has a lower bound:

$$
\Sigma\ \ge\ \Sigma_{\min}\ =\ \frac{L_{\min}^{2}\,\rho_{s}\,e_{s}}{\kappa\,\Delta T\,\tau_{\mathrm{op}}}\ >\ 0 \tag{III-8.39}
$$

The source of $L_{\min}$ is structural mechanics (bearing self-weight and external loads) and manufacturing technique, not thermodynamics. This is why the lower bound on the shell must be downgraded to a proposition: **the source of the lower bound is mechanics and technique; thermodynamics provides only the relation between $R$ and $\dot E_{\mathrm{core}}$.**

**Proposition 8.12 (Lower Bound on the Thermal-Shell Ratio, Conditional Version)**

- **Premises**: (i) the core is approximately spherical, and heat is conducted out one-dimensionally along the radial direction; (ii) $\kappa$, $\rho_{s}$, and $e_{s}$ are constants over the temperature-rise range; (iii) the permitted temperature rise $\Delta T$ is given and does not vary with scale; (iv) $L_{\min}>0$; (v) $\tau_{\mathrm{op}}$ is given; (vi) convection and radiation are neglected (they can only make the required $R$ smaller, so this proposition gives a conservative estimate).
- **Result**: $\Sigma\ge L_{\min}^{2}\rho_{s}e_{s}/(\kappa\Delta T\tau_{\mathrm{op}})>0$. This lower bound is determined only by the material parameters, the permitted temperature rise, and the operating period, and is independent of the core power and the core scale. The step of linking it to computing density requires building a separate model that contains $\dot E_{\mathrm{core}}$, and this proposition does not claim it. What this section can give is therefore only this: the shell **energy ratio** $\Sigma$ has a positive lower bound $\Sigma_{\min}$ independent of power and scale (a weak lower bound, not a predicted value), rather than rising monotonically with density.
- **Assumptions relied on**: The geometry is spherical; the material parameters do not vary with temperature; the shell bears only the heat-dissipation function (if it also bears structure, shielding, or maintenance access, $\Sigma$ can only be larger).
- **Failure conditions**: (a) $L_{\min}\to0$ (thin films, self-supporting structures), and the lower bound disappears; (b) active cooling (liquid cooling, phase change, heat pipes) makes the conduction model of Eq. (III-8.38) inapplicable; (c) the core is non-spherical, the area-to-volume ratio rises, and the constraint relaxes; (d) $\tau_{\mathrm{op}}$ is extremely short, and the shell heat capacity rather than conduction dominates.

**Check.** Taking $L_{\min}=10^{-2}\ \mathrm{m}$, $\rho_{s}e_{s}\approx2.5\times10^{9}\ \mathrm{J/m^{3}}$, and $\kappa\Delta T\tau_{\mathrm{op}}\approx3\times10^{9}$ gives $\Sigma_{\min}\approx8\times10^{-5}$, which is lower by three to nearly four orders of magnitude than the share of cooling and support energy consumption in actual facilities ($10\%$–$40\%$). **This shows that $\Sigma_{\min}$ is a weak lower bound and not a prediction of the actual share**: the actual share is determined by engineering margins, redundant configurations, and the heat-dissipation mode, and is far higher than this thermodynamic-plus-mechanical lower bound.

**Falsification condition.** No trend-based condition is set here — §8.11 has already disclaimed any claim that the share rises monotonically with density. Only the bound itself is testable: if the measured share of cooling and support falls below $\Sigma_{\min}$, that bound is falsified.

## Chapter 9 — Corollaries, Hypotheses, and Limitations

This chapter records the corollaries, hypotheses, and unfinished items whose strength is insufficient but which should still be stated, and assigns their levels.

### 9.1 Candidate Corollaries

All three corollaries are derived from the preceding propositions under additional conditions and have not been independently tested; their level is **Candidate Corollary**.

**Corollary 9.1 (Diminishing Marginal Returns on Educational Input)** Educational input is the energy integral of decoder training, $E_{\mathrm{edu}}=\int_{0}^{T_{\mathrm{train}}}\dot E_{\mathrm{causal}}^{(\mathrm{training})}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t$. As the decoder approaches a local optimum under the given architecture, the gradient term tends to zero, $\mathrm{d}\eta_{D}/\mathrm{d}E_{\mathrm{edu}}\to0$, so there exists $E_{\mathrm{edu}}^{*}$ such that $\partial V/\partial E_{\mathrm{edu}}=0$; beyond it, the viability-potential return of continued education is negative. **Parent proposition**: Proposition 8.1. **Measurable consequence**: educational input and innovation output show an inverted-U relation. **Failure condition**: if education also changes the architecture (learning how to learn), the gradient does not tend to zero and the inverted U does not appear.

**Corollary 9.2 (Scale Effect of Computation Outsourcing)** Let structure $i$ concentrate its high-energy modules at node $j$, with the network total cost $E_{\mathrm{eff}}^{(\mathrm{net})}=\sum_{i}E_{\mathrm{eff},i}+E_{\mathrm{coordination}}$; when $E_{\mathrm{coordination}}<\sum_{i}E_{\mathrm{causal}}^{(i)}$, outsourcing lowers the average cost. **Parent proposition**: Proposition 2.1. **Measurable consequence**: shared infrastructure lowers the participants' average effective cost. **Failure condition**: coordination cost grows superlinearly with scale, or outsourcing creates external dependence for critical capabilities — the gains of outsourcing are continuous and its risks are discrete, so comparing by averages systematically overestimates outsourcing.

**Corollary 9.3 (The Direction of Knowledge Compression)** After the decoder is upgraded ($D\to D'$, $\eta_{D'}>\eta_{D}$), the amount of latent information needed to complete the same task falls, so $E_{\mathrm{causal}}^{(\mathrm{new})}<E_{\mathrm{causal}}^{(\mathrm{old})}$; the direction of knowledge evolution is to raise $\eta_{D}$ and lower the $E_{\mathrm{causal}}$ per unit task. **Parent proposition**: Proposition 2.1. **Measurable consequence**: when a higher-level theory explains the same phenomenon, the understanding energy consumption is lower than for a lower-level theory. **Failure condition**: "the same task" does not hold in cross-domain comparisons; or the learning cost of the higher-level theory exceeds the running cost it saves.

### 9.2 Hypotheses

**Hypothesis 9.1 (Innovation Window)** The relation between the candidate-signal production rate and the cognitive state of the system is an inverted U. When $\eta_{D}\to0$ the system cannot recognize signals; when $\eta_{D}\to1$ the virtual viability potential inflates and the endogenous time horizon collapses, so the incentive to produce signals is lost; in the middle region the deviation is moderate, with both motive and capacity. **Falsification condition**: the candidate-signal production rates of children, youth, and periods of scientific revolution do not show an inverted U; or $\eta_{D}$ has no significant correlation with the production rate. **Known counterexample pressure**: it conflicts with "experts break through more easily"; a possible reconciliation is that experts have a high production rate within their domain and a low production rate across domains.

### 9.3 Unfinished Derivations and Uncalibrated Parameters

Seven derivations are explicitly unfinished. **The closed-form solution of 8.1**: Proposition 8.1 gives only the bang-bang structure, and the switching time requires the specific form of $I_{\mathrm{causal}}(c)$. **The calibration of $\kappa_{\mathrm{orig}}$ in 8.2**: the proportionality coefficient of Eq. (III-4.6) has never been independently measured. **The functional forms of 8.4**: $\eta_{D}(D)$, $E_{\mathrm{causal}}(D)$, and $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}(D)$ have only qualitative assumptions, and concavity has not been verified. **The $k_{\mathrm{sw}}$ prediction of 8.6**: the formula for the critical switching count can be computed, but the premise $k_{\mathrm{sw}}>k_{\mathrm{sw}}^{c}$ does not hold at typical parameters, so (III-8.24) serves only as a failure registration and not as a prediction. **The measurement of $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}$ in 8.8**: the three paths differ by more than $0.05$, corresponding to one to two generations. **The form of $\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{self}}$ in 8.9**: the three-factor product compresses resolution and salience into one scalar, and whether the loss is critical has not been analyzed. **The prior $\pi_{0}$ in 8.10**: the prior cannot be estimated from data.

There are ten groups of uncalibrated parameters: $\beta_{\mathrm{erase}}$ and $\xi$ (varying by architecture and by process), $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}$ and $\eta_{c}$ (no independent measurement), $\Delta_{\mathrm{crit}}$ and $\Delta_{\max}$ (never measured), $\kappa_{\mathrm{orig}}$ (unknown), $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}$ (estimated at $0.75$–$0.80$), $k_{\mathrm{m}}$ (no measurement), $E_{a}$ and $D_{\mathrm{crit}}$ (material-dependent), $L_{\min}$ (process-dependent), $\psi(\rho)$ (no functional form), and $p_{0}$ (depends on the candidate set). What they have in common is that the direction of the conclusion depends on their existence and the numerical value of the conclusion depends on their size; before calibration, all quantitative conclusions of this volume should be read as order-of-magnitude estimates.

### 9.4 What This Volume Does Not Say

**What this volume does not say.** It does not prove that "humans are irreplaceable" — what it proves is that physical embedding, within a specific model, provides a source of candidate signals, and that this source can be partly replaced by self-made noise, external criteria, and better synthetic methods. It does not prove that "AI necessarily degrades" — what it proves is that when training is closed and recursive and the retention rate stays below $1$, effective information decays multiplicatively, a condition that often fails in reality. It does not prove that "the higher the complexity the better" — what it gives is a trade-off, and in many cases the optimum lies on the boundary.

**Usage recommendations.** The four engineering indicators ($R$, $k_{\mathrm{sw}}^{c}$, $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{gen}}$, $I_{V,\mathrm{crit}}$) can all be used for engineering estimation, but the parameters are uncalibrated, and the error would be very large if they were used as a budget table. An engineer needs only three formulas — $R=C_{\mathrm{comp}}/E_{\mathrm{build}}$, $\dot E_{\mathrm{heat}}\ge\xi\beta_{\mathrm{erase}}k_BT_{\mathrm{thermo}}\ln2\cdot\dot I_{\mathrm{active}}$, and $\Sigma\ge L_{\min}^{2}\rho_{s}e_{s}/(\kappa\Delta T\tau_{\mathrm{op}})$ — and all of them require the architecture parameters to be calibrated first; a researcher should first find the predictions in Table 7.1 that are relevant to the field and then work backwards to the parameters that need calibration.

### A.4 Four Groups of Traps in Symbol Use

This volume has a large number of symbols, of which four groups share a form but denote different quantities. They are collected here as a last check before taking values.

**First group: the four usages of ε.** $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}$ (**compilation cost per bit**, $\mathrm{J/bit}$; this is what is meant when no subscript is written), $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{lr}}$ (**learning rate**, with the same dimension as the parameters), $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{res}}$ (**prediction residual**), $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{em}}$ (radiative emissivity, dimensionless). There is also a group $\varepsilon_{\eta},\varepsilon_{E},\varepsilon_{\Delta}$ (elasticities, dimensionless log–log slopes). **The main text of this volume has no unsubscripted ε** — wherever ε appears, check the subscript first.

**Second group: the five $k$'s.** $k_{B}$ (Boltzmann constant, $\mathrm{J/K}$), $k_{\mathrm{m}}$ (maintenance cost per unit information, $\mathrm{J/bit}$), $k_{\mathrm{sw}}$ (expected number of topological switches, dimensionless), $k_{\mathrm{sw}}^{c}$ (critical switching count, dimensionless), $k_{D}$ (energy penalty coefficient of the drift equation). The five have different dimensions, and mixing them directly leads to errors of orders of magnitude; $k_{\mathrm{sw}}$ and $k_{\mathrm{sw}}^{c}$ differ only by a superscript, so when comparing them one must confirm whether a quantity is the observed value or the threshold.

**Third group: the three $\sigma$'s.** $\sigma$ (noise intensity of the decoupling-deviation equation, $\mathrm{bit/J}$), $\sigma_{\mathrm{sw}}$ (switching function of the optimal control problem, with the same dimension as the objective functional), $\sigma_{\mathrm{SB}}$ (Stefan–Boltzmann constant, $\mathrm{W/(m^{2}K^{4})}$). The latter two carry subscripts; the first does not, and is identified by where it occurs — the one that follows $\mathrm{d}\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}/\mathrm{d}t$ is the noise, and the one that follows $\partial\mathcal{H}/\partial u$ is the switching function.

**Fourth group: the four $\rho$'s.** $\rho_{\mathrm{disc}}$ (discount rate of the objective functional, $\mathrm{s^{-1}}$, see §8.1), $\rho$ (competitive pressure index, dimensionless, see §8.5), $\rho_{s}$ (shell material density, $\mathrm{kg/m^{3}}$, see §8.11), $\rho_{\mathrm{bit}}$ (information density, $\mathrm{bit/m^{3}}$). Wherever the main text refers to the competitive pressure index, the symbol is always immediately followed by the words "competitive pressure" and is not used alone.

**Three-step checking procedure.** Every comparison of the form "some quantity exceeds some threshold" can be run through as follows: step one, write the units on both sides and cancel them term by term; step two, if they are not equal after cancellation, suspect the definition before the calculation; step three, if they are equal but the ratio changes with the choice of units, it was never dimensionless to begin with and cannot be used as a threshold. $V$ falls precisely outside step three — it carries bit, is not of the same kind as 1, and cannot be compared directly with 1.

The common lesson of the four groups of traps is this: **a symbol clash is not a typesetting problem but a reasoning problem.** If the learning rate and the per-bit cost were written as the same $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{c}}$, the dimensions of Section 8.1 would immediately fail to close; if the medium density and the competitive pressure were written as the same $\rho$, the shell ratio of Section 8.11 would be miscalculated by orders of magnitude. Writing them apart costs longer symbols, and the gain is that every step of checking can be carried out.

<!-- VOLUME Ⅳ ｜ Decoder Game Theory and Alignment Engineering -->
## Volume IV — Decoder Game Theory and Alignment Engineering

> **This volume = Volume IV.**

## Abstract

Traditional analysis places the observer outside the equations. This is valid at the scale of inert matter, where the measured object does not change its behavior because it is measured. When the object is a living organism, a social system, or artificial intelligence, the approximation fails, because their perception is active extraction. This volume promotes the decoder $D$ (the physical mechanism by which a persistent structure extracts effective causal information from latent configurations) to an independent dynamical variable, and answers three questions: how decoders evolve, when they fail, and how several decoders form a stable structure of coexistence or cooperation.

The method has three layers. **One-dimensional quality dynamics** compresses extraction capability into the scalar $\mathcal{Q}_D=\eta_DI_{\max}(D)$, which can be computed by hand, at the cost of discarding structural information. **Payoff structure and horizon** are valued on the common accounting window $T_0$ and do not contain the endogenous time horizon; the horizon enters only through the discount factor $e^{-t_{\mathrm{dec}}/T_{\mathrm{pred}}}$, and without this separation the critical horizon is an implicit equation. **The construction-cost boundary** rises exponentially with complexity, and yields a static boundary rather than a dynamic destination.

Main results. **Proposition**: the quality equation is piecewise stable. When $\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}>\delta_{\mathrm{decay}}$ the zero point is unstable and the positive fixed point is stable; when the inequality is reversed the zero point is stable and the system dies out; when the two are equal there is only algebraic decay. **Proposition**: under complete monitoring, a grim trigger, stationary payoffs, and enforceable punishment, cooperation is a subgame-perfect equilibrium if and only if $T_{\mathrm{pred}}\ge T_{\mathrm{crit}}$. **Proposition**: the conquest game is zero-sum at the share layer and negative-sum at the cost layer, and $\Delta V_A=-\Delta V_B$ holds only as $\Delta E_{\mathrm{conflict}}\to0$. **Proposition**: superadditivity is conditional on four premises, and the addition of information across subjects is a modeling convention. **Proposition**: a meta-decoder equilibrium must hold against all feasible deviations, with enforceable punishment. **Proposition**: the exponential law of construction cost is assumption A3.1, and $C_{\max}$ is only a static boundary. **Hypothesis**: the direction in which cross-decoder barriers affect ethical concern is measurable, and the magnitude has not been calibrated.

**Scope statement.** $V$ is a descriptive quantity and not a normative criterion for dealing with real individuals; this volume does not claim that decoders necessarily converge to any terminal state; the game-theoretic conclusions are limited to repeated games with complete monitoring and observable payoffs; except for passages tagged as propositions, passages involving artificial intelligence are engineering recommendations.

**Keywords**: Structure Recursive Viability Dynamics; decoder quality; topological transition; repeated games; subgame-perfect equilibrium; negative-sum cost; alignment engineering

***

## Chapter 1 — The Problem and Its Placement

Excluding the observer from the equations is an approximation with conditions of applicability; this chapter states these three conditions and the boundaries of the two models.

### 1.1 What This Volume Answers and What It Does Not

This volume answers three questions. **The evolution of the decoder itself**: in ordinary periods without topological transitions, in which direction the extraction capability of an existing decoder changes, at what level it stops, and under what conditions it degrades. Only because capability has its own growth rate and decay rate does "investing in cognition" become an option. **When a decoder fails**: either the capability degrades until it can no longer sustain extraction, or the capability has not degraded but the internal evaluation has decoupled from the objective stock; the phenotypes are similar and the mechanisms opposite, so the former is repaired by repairing capability and the latter by repairing the reference. **How multiple decoders form a stable structure**: coexistence, cooperation, conquest, and mutual destruction each have a place in the payoff structure; this volume asks which form is an equilibrium given the payoff structure, whether the equilibrium holds given the horizon, and whether it is affordable given the construction cost.

This volume does not answer four classes of questions. **It does not answer the question of consciousness**: when it speaks of a "cognitive decoder" it means an extraction mechanism that has a predictive model and whose behavior can be measured by a third party. **It does not answer normative questions**: it can say "under a given payoff structure, defection is an equilibrium," but it cannot say "therefore defection is right." **It does not answer quantitative predictions for specific systems**: the equations contain several parameters that have not been calibrated, and until calibration is complete only directions and ordinal relations are given. **It does not answer incomplete-information games**: all game-theoretic conclusions rest on the assumption of complete monitoring, and this assumption is least reliable precisely between humans and strong artificial intelligence.

### 1.2 Evidence Levels, Two Models, and Notation

This volume grades its conclusions into four levels by the completeness of the derivation; section headings and sub-conclusions in the main text carry tags.

| Tag | Conditions of use | Must be accompanied by |
| :---------- | :---------------------------- | :------------------- |
| **Theorem** | Premises fully listed, derivation complete, conclusion strictly valid under those premises | Premises, result, derivation, domain of validity |
| **Proposition** | A formal statement exists, but the premises are partly to be calibrated, or one step of the derivation depends on an unproven modeling assumption | Premises, result, assumptions relied on, failure conditions |
| **Candidate Corollary** | Derived from a proposition under additional conditions, not independently tested | Parent proposition, additional conditions, measurable consequences |
| **Hypothesis** | An empirical or mechanistic conjecture without formal derivation | Statement, supporting rationale, falsification condition |

This volume has no **Theorem**-level conclusions: every formal conclusion depends on at least one unproven modeling assumption — scalar compression, payoff observability, the exponential form of the construction cost, or the additivity of cross-subject information. Only physical lower bounds such as Landauer's principle are at the **Theorem** level, and this volume uses it only as an order-of-magnitude reference.

**Two models, two levels.** The main model is the decoder quality equation (Equation (IV-3.3)):

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{Q}_D}{\mathrm{d}t}=\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}\mathcal{Q}_D\left(1-\frac{\mathcal{Q}_D}{\mathcal{Q}_{D,\max}}\right)-\delta_{\mathrm{decay}}\mathcal{Q}_D+\kappa_{\mathrm{topo}}I_{\mathrm{orig}}(t)\,\delta(t-t_{\mathrm{jump}})
$$

The state is the one-dimensional scalar $\mathcal{Q}_D$ ($\mathrm{bit}$), and the critical structure is the rate ratio $\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}/\delta_{\mathrm{decay}}$. The auxiliary model is the $I$–$V$ feedback subsystem, with a two-dimensional state $(I,V)$ and parameters that include the self-referential coupling strength $\chi$; it serves only to illustrate "how the internal evaluation is amplified by itself." Three statements hold throughout the volume: **the main model does not contain $\chi$, and the auxiliary model does not contain $\mathcal{Q}_D$**, so neither can be substituted into the other; **$\chi_{\mathrm{c}}$ is not used as a criterion**, because $\chi$ has no independent measurement method, and wherever a measurable criterion is needed the equilibrium deviation $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}^{*}=\sigma/(\alpha_{\mathrm{L}}I_{\mathrm{net}})$ is compared with the tolerance $\Delta_{\mathrm{crit}}$; **the critical parameters of the two models are not interchangeable**.

**Notation discipline.** Time variables are not interchanged: $T_{\mathrm{struct}}$, $T_{\mathrm{causal}}$, $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$, $T_{\mathrm{min}}$, $T_{\mathrm{env}}$, and the common accounting window of the game chapters is written $T_0$ (a protocol constant, not an alias for any of them); a bare $T$ is not used. $V$ retains the bit ($\mathrm{bit\cdot s/J}$), and only $v=V/V_0$ is dimensionless — precisely because $V$ carries bits, $V$ across subjects cannot be added arbitrarily. For any criterion of the "exceeds a threshold" kind, both sides are reduced to the same dimension before they are compared.

### 1.3 When Treating the Decoder as a Fixed Background Is Valid

Three conditions holding together define the "inert-matter scale," and they are the source of the legitimacy with which classical physics excludes the observer. **Condition one, measurement does not act back on the object**; **condition two, extraction does not change the extractor**, so there is no loop of "the more it sees, the better it sees" or "the more it sees, the blinder it becomes"; **condition three, within the observation window the environment and the architecture are stationary**, parameter drift is negligible, and the capacity ceiling is not reset. If any one of them fails, the equations must contain $D$ explicitly. The three domains each break different conditions:

| Domain | Condition broken | Correction needed |
| :------------------ | :---------------- | :----------------------- |
| Living organisms (individual scale) | Conditions one and three | Drift equation + capacity ceiling |
| Social systems and institutions | Conditions one and two | Multi-subject coupling + game payoffs |
| Artificial intelligence | Conditions one, two, and three | All of the above, plus the construction-cost constraint |

***

## Chapter 2 — The Ontology and Taxonomy of Decoders

This chapter gives "decoder" an operational definition, distinguishes it from the executor and the objective function, and gives the accounting rules for cross-decoder comparison.

### 2.1 Definition and Net Effective Causal Information

**Definition 2.1 (Decoder)** $D$ is the physical mechanism by which a persistent structure extracts effective causal information from latent configurations $I_{\mathrm{latent}}$. "Persistent structure" means an entity that maintains its own identity within the observation window and can be distinguished from the environment (denoted PS); "latent configuration" means the causal possibilities that objectively exist in the environment and have not been activated by that structure. There are three implicit requirements: $D$ must be physical (having a substrate and consuming energy); the input must be latent (if it is already fully activated, $D$ degenerates into a transparent channel); the output must be effective (an extraction that changes only internal variables without affecting subsequent processes is not counted in $I_{\mathrm{net}}$).

**Net effective causal information** is composed by channel energy weights:

$$
I_{\mathrm{net}}=\varphi_{\mathrm{s}}I_{\mathrm{struct}}+\varphi_{\mathrm{c}}I_{\mathrm{causal}},\qquad \varphi_{\mathrm{s}}=\frac{E_{\mathrm{struct}}}{E_{\mathrm{eff}}},\quad \varphi_{\mathrm{c}}=\frac{E_{\mathrm{causal}}}{E_{\mathrm{eff}}},\quad \varphi_{\mathrm{s}}+\varphi_{\mathrm{c}}=1
$$

$I_{\mathrm{struct}}$ is the distinguishable difference of the structure relative to the background, $I_{\mathrm{causal}}$ is the rarity of the effect that the structure produces relative to the control, and $E_{\mathrm{eff}}=E_{\mathrm{struct}}+E_{\mathrm{causal}}$ (the convention of the main text of this volume). Volume Z's three-way decomposition is connected to it by $E_{\mathrm{struct}}=E_{\mathrm{barrier}}+E_{\mathrm{survival}}$ (see Volume I §3.2 and Volume II §5); the two are not parallel definitions. This weighted sum is a **modeling convention**, not an information-theoretic conclusion: the two channels differ in reading protocol, background, and definition of effect, and measuring both in bits does not constitute a proof of additivity — two rulers both graduated in millimeters do not prove that they measure the same length. Therefore $I_{\mathrm{net}}$ is used as a numerical value only under the same reading protocol, the same background, and the same observation window, after in-domain calibration is complete; otherwise it serves only as an ordinal indicator.

**Decoding efficiency** $\eta_D\in[0,1]$ is the fraction actually activated, $I_{\mathrm{active}}=\eta_DI_{\mathrm{latent}}$; it does not define $I_{\mathrm{net}}$ — the former is what fraction is activated, and the latter is how the activated part is composed across the two channels. External shocks are divided by their net effect on $I_{\mathrm{net}}$ into $I^{+}$, $I^{-}$, and $I^{0}$; this is a classification by **receiver effect** and not by content: the same bundle of signals can fall into different classes for different decoders.

### 2.2 The Four Qualification Conditions (the Convention of This Volume)

**Definition 2.2** A physical mechanism constitutes a **decoder holder** (that is, the physical mechanism that performs the decoding functional link) in the game-theoretic and dynamical sense only if it satisfies all of the **four qualification conditions** (the criteria are given in this volume, see the table below; no external text is relied upon, and none of the four may be omitted):

| Condition | Criterion | Required observable | Failure phenotype |
| :----------- | :----------------- | :-------------- | :------------- |
| 1. Self-maintained identifiable boundary | The structure maintains an identifiable boundary within the observation window | Identifiability of the boundary | No boundary; classified under $D=\varnothing$ |
| 2. Selective extraction | Extracts only the subset of signals relevant to the system's persistence, with a measurable suppression ratio | Response gain ratio of target signals to irrelevant signals | All channels received with equal weight, equivalent to no decoding |
| 3. State update triggered by extraction | The extracted difference actually changes the subsequent internal state or behavior | Change in the action distribution after intervening on the extraction channel | Extraction without action; information idling |
| 4. Causal significance for one's own persistence | The update participates in maintaining the structure's own persistence | Coupling between the update and the viability indicator | Update unrelated to persistence; does not constitute a holder |

**Updatability is not a qualification condition but an additional attribute.** An ion channel fixed by genes and not reconstructed within the life cycle still satisfies the four conditions above and therefore constitutes a decoder holder — that it cannot be reconstructed during operation shows only that it belongs to the "intergenerational update" side, not that it is not a $D$. Crystals and rocks have no self-maintained boundary, do not satisfy condition 1, and are classified under $D=\varnothing$ — this convention separates "having structure" from "having decoding." Another common error is to treat an executor as a decoder: its input is a command rather than a latent configuration, and the criterion differs in the direction of condition 3.

### 2.3 Two Classes of Decoders and the Activity Criterion

| | Physical decoder $D_{\mathrm{phys}}$ | Cognitive decoder $D_{\mathrm{cog}}$ |
| :---------------- | :----------------- | :------------------------ |
| Nature | Selective extraction with a self-maintained boundary, no predictive model | An active interface with a predictive model, able to model its own extraction mode |
| Efficiency | $\eta_D$ low and approximately constant | $\eta_D>0$ and can rise with learning |
| Time horizon | $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$ pinned to the lower bound $T_{\mathrm{min}}$ | Has an extendable $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$ |
| Updatability (**additional attribute, not a qualification condition**) | Not satisfied during operation, satisfied across generations | Satisfied during operation |
| Typical examples | Ion channels, bacterial chemotaxis, plant phototropism | Nervous systems, **artificial intelligence systems whose self-maintained boundary holds (such as embodied systems)**, institutional systems |

The key difference is not complexity but **whether a predictive model is contained**: one that contains a predictive model can prepare for inputs that have not yet occurred, its $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$ can be lengthened and its discount factor can approach 1, and it can also model itself and so give rise to self-referential coupling, in which the decoupling deviation acquires an amplification channel. Institutional systems belong to the cognitive decoder side (they contain explicit expectation structures such as contracts, precedents, and conventions, and can reconstruct rules after a crisis); those that act only according to a fixed charter fall back to the physical decoder side.

**Definition 2.3 (Active Persistence)** The following must hold simultaneously: the internal state is controllable, the energy flow is adjustable, and $T_{\mathrm{pred}}>0$. Rocks and planets satisfy none of the three; a star fails condition 1; a thermostat satisfies the first two but its horizon is extremely short. **Activity is not purposiveness**: the game-theoretic conclusions of this volume depend only on the payoff structure.

### 2.4 The Decoder's Own Viability Potential and the Rules for Cross-Decoder Comparison

The decoder is itself a persistent structure, and the low-order approximation of its viability potential is $V(D)\approx I_{\mathrm{net}}(D)T(D)/E_{\mathrm{eff}}(D)$; the two-channel form is

$$
V_{\mathrm{obj}}(D)=\frac{I_{\mathrm{struct}}T_{\mathrm{struct}}}{E_{\mathrm{struct}}}+\frac{I_{\mathrm{causal}}T_{\mathrm{causal}}}{E_{\mathrm{causal}}} \tag{IV-2.1}
$$

Each term has dimension $\mathrm{bit\cdot s/J}$; the additivity here is the addition of the viability potentials of the two channels under the same holder and the same observation window, which is a separate question from the additivity of $I_{\mathrm{net}}$.

**Rules for cross-decoder comparison and summation.** The $V$ of different holders cannot be added or subtracted directly. First, **the reading protocols differ**: the same event is read as a 3 bit positive effect at A and as a 5 bit negative effect at B; both readings are compliant, and adding them is meaningless. Second, **the observation windows differ**: $T_{\mathrm{struct}}$ and $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$ are both endogenous, so different horizons mean different integration intervals. Third, **the cost sharing differs**: the same $E_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is counted twice by the two sides, and the total cost is overestimated. Therefore a cross-subject sum must first declare a **common boundary**, a **reference protocol**, an **observation window** (unified to $T_0$), and **cost de-duplication**; when these cannot all be established at once, only a qualitative comparison is made — "A is better than B" is allowed, $\sum_iV_i$ is not.

**Where the three internal quantities stand.** $V_{\mathrm{obj}}$ is computed from externally verifiable cost and time and can be recomputed by a third party; $V^{\mathrm{virt}}$ is the internal model's evaluation of its own persistence status and can inflate under self-referential feedback; $V^{\mathrm{val}}$ is the target the system actually optimizes, formed from $V^{\mathrm{virt}}$ weighted by $w_{\mathrm{s}}$ and $w_{\mathrm{c}}$ (summing to 1), and when $w_{\mathrm{s}}\to0$ the optimization target is almost unrelated to physical persistence.

***

## Chapter 3 — Core Equations of Decoder Dynamics

### 3.1 Drift and the Quality Equation

The decoder drifts along the gradient of the viability potential in the parameter space $\Theta$ (step size $\varepsilon_{\mathrm{lr}}$; $k_D$ is dimensionless, and $I_0=1\ \mathrm{bit}$, $E_0=1\ \mathrm{J}$ serve only to close the dimensions):

$$
D_{n+1}=D_n+\varepsilon_{\mathrm{lr}}\left(\nabla_{\Theta}I_{\mathrm{net}}-k_D\,\frac{I_0}{E_0}\,\nabla_{\Theta}E_{\mathrm{eff}}\right) \tag{IV-3.1}
$$

The two gradients have different dimensions (bit versus J) and cannot be added directly, so the quality scalar is introduced:

$$
\mathcal{Q}_D(t)\equiv\eta_D(t)\cdot I_{\max}\big(D(t)\big) \tag{IV-3.2}
$$

that is, the product of the efficiency and the capacity ceiling ($\mathrm{bit}$). A high-selectivity, low-capacity decoder and a low-selectivity, high-capacity decoder can give the same $\mathcal{Q}_D$, so the conclusions are restricted to a single architectural lineage. The three forces are:

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}\mathcal{Q}_D}{\mathrm{d}t}=\underbrace{\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}\,\mathcal{Q}_D\left(1-\frac{\mathcal{Q}_D}{\mathcal{Q}_{D,\max}}\right)}_{\text{endogenous recursive growth}}-\underbrace{\delta_{\mathrm{decay}}\,\mathcal{Q}_D}_{\text{natural decay}}+\underbrace{\kappa_{\mathrm{topo}}\,I_{\mathrm{orig}}(t)\,\delta(t-t_{\mathrm{jump}})}_{\text{topological-transition injection}} \tag{IV-3.3}
$$

$\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}$ and $\delta_{\mathrm{decay}}$ have unit $\mathrm{s^{-1}}$; $\mathcal{Q}_{D,\max}=I_{\max}(D_n)$ is the capacity ceiling of the current architecture, reset after each transition. The first term is the Logistic form of the flywheel, with positive feedback saturating at the capacity ceiling; the second term is thermodynamic passive degradation. **Landauer's principle does not give the numerical value of $\delta_{\mathrm{decay}}$**: it constrains the energy per erased bit, $k_BT_{\mathrm{thermo}}\ln2$, serves only as an order-of-magnitude reference for the maintenance power, and differs in granularity from the baseline depreciation rate $\gamma_0$.

**Fixed points and piecewise stability (Proposition).** When the pulse term is zero, the equation reduces to Eq. (IV-3.4); the linear coefficient at the zero point is $\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}-\delta_{\mathrm{decay}}$, and the fixed points are $0$ and $\mathcal{Q}_{D,\max}(1-\delta_{\mathrm{decay}}/\mu_{\mathrm{rec}})$, the latter lying in the physical domain only when $\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}>\delta_{\mathrm{decay}}$:

$$
\dot{\mathcal{Q}}_D=(\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}-\delta_{\mathrm{decay}})\,\mathcal{Q}_D-\frac{\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}}{\mathcal{Q}_{D,\max}}\,\mathcal{Q}_D^{2} \tag{IV-3.4}
$$

| Parameter range | $\mathcal{Q}_D=0$ | Positive fixed point | Long-term behavior |
| :-------- | :-------------------- | :-------------- | :--------------- |
| $\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}>\delta_{\mathrm{decay}}$ | Unstable | $\mathcal{Q}_{D,\max}\left(1-\dfrac{\delta_{\mathrm{decay}}}{\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}}\right)>0$, stable | Converges to a positive level |
| $\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}<\delta_{\mathrm{decay}}$ | Globally stable | Does not exist (solves to a negative value) | Decreases monotonically to 0, at an exponential rate |
| $\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}=\delta_{\mathrm{decay}}$ | Asymptotically stable on the physical domain (algebraic convergence; semi-stable on $\mathbb{R}$) | Coincides with the zero point | $\mathcal{Q}_D(t)=\dfrac{\mathcal{Q}_D(0)}{1+\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}\mathcal{Q}_D(0)t/\mathcal{Q}_{D,\max}}$ |

**Premises**: the pulse term is zero; the parameters are constant within the observation window; $\mathcal{Q}_D\ge0$. **Result**: as in the table above; the necessary and sufficient condition for the positive fixed point to exist is $\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}>\delta_{\mathrm{decay}}$. **Assumptions relied on**: quality can be scalarized; the growth rate depends only on $\mathcal{Q}_D$. **Failure conditions**: if $\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}$ declines as $\mathcal{Q}_D$ declines, the analysis must be recomputed; if $I_{\mathrm{orig}}$ is a continuous flow, the equation is non-autonomous.

The equal case gives algebraic decay rather than "instability": $\mathcal{Q}_D$ falls to zero without a characteristic time constant, and "half-life" is meaningless here.

**Two mechanisms of D-lock-in.** (**Reminder on the same name with different meanings**: the "D-lock-in" of Volume II §4.6 refers to "decoder gradient saturation + insufficient budget" and is not the same mechanism as the "approach lock-in / decay domain" of this section; this branch in this volume can be called **quality lock-in**. When these two kinds of lock-in are cited across volumes, the volume number must be given, so that the same name is not mistaken for the same meaning.) **Approach lock-in**: when $\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}/\delta_{\mathrm{decay}}\to1^{+}$, $\mathcal{Q}_D^{*}\to0^{+}$, and the system stops at an extremely low but strictly positive extraction level, characterized by $\dot{\mathcal{Q}}_D\to0$ while $\mathcal{Q}_D>0$; **a small adjustment of parameters is enough to exit, with no topological transition needed** — the exact meaning of "$\nabla D\to0$" is that the gradient vanishes, not that the capability vanishes. **Decay domain**: when $\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}<\delta_{\mathrm{decay}}$ there is no positive fixed point, $\mathcal{Q}_D$ falls monotonically to zero with no lower plateau, and exiting requires an external injection that lifts $\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}$ back above $\delta_{\mathrm{decay}}$. The former runs slowly; the latter cannot run at all: for approach lock-in, incentives such as lowering the maintenance cost may be effective; for the decay domain, the same intervention only prolongs the decline. A third case ($\mathcal{Q}_D$ stays at zero while the system is still operating) must be excluded; that is a decoder that has been abandoned.

**Gating of $\mathcal{T}$ transitions.** Three conditions must hold simultaneously:

$$
I_{\mathrm{orig}}(t_{\mathrm{jump}})>0\ \wedge\ E_{\mathrm{available}}>E_{\mathrm{build}}\ \wedge\ \kappa_{\mathrm{topo}}\,\tilde I_{\mathrm{orig}}\,\frac{\partial V}{\partial\mathcal{Q}_D}>L_{\mathrm{lev,min}}\,E_{\mathrm{build}} \tag{IV-3.5}
$$

$L_{\mathrm{lev}}\equiv\Delta V/E_{\mathrm{build}}$ has dimension $\mathrm{bit\cdot s/J^{2}}$, and after multiplication by $E_{\mathrm{build}}$ it has the same dimension as the left-hand side, $\mathrm{bit\cdot s/J}$. An operational trap: $I_{\mathrm{orig}}$ is a quantity that can be confirmed only **after the fact**; before a transition only a proxy can be observed (Eq. (IV-3.6)), and the significance level $\alpha_0$ must be pre-registered; a proxy reading above the threshold does not mean that there is truly new information.

$$
\tilde I_{\mathrm{orig}}\equiv-\log_2\Pr_{B_n}\big(\varepsilon_{\mathrm{res}}\ge\varepsilon_{\mathrm{obs}}\big) \tag{IV-3.6}
$$

### 3.2 Topological Transitions and the Complexity Threshold

$$
D_n(I_{\mathrm{orig}})\approx0,\qquad \exists\,D_{n+1}:D_{n+1}(I_{\mathrm{orig}})\gg0 \tag{IV-3.7}
$$

The criterion for "approximately zero" is the signal-to-noise ratio, and the determination requires a baseline control. A transition requires a one-time cost $E_{\mathrm{build}}$ and resets the capacity ceiling; the pulse records the instantaneous increase in the amount of information, and the capacity reset records the change in the ceiling, and the two must not be counted twice. Complexity is defined as

$$
C_D\equiv\theta_{\mathrm{depth}}\,D_{\mathrm{depth}}+\theta_{\mathrm{cap}}\log_2\!\left(\frac{I_{\max}(D)}{I_{\mathrm{ref}}}\right),\qquad I_{\mathrm{ref}}=\log_2N_{\mathrm{state}} \tag{IV-3.8}
$$

$N_{\mathrm{state}}$ is the minimum number of distinguishable states of a passive structure. Three points must be stated: **the base is unified to 2**, otherwise the derivative formula differs by a factor $\ln10/\ln2\approx3.32$; **the reference quantity must be an amount of information and not a number of states**, otherwise the ratio is not necessarily dimensionless; **the normalization coefficients are calibration parameters**. The incremental form is Eq. (IV-3.9):

$$
C_{D,n+1}=C_{D,n}+\theta_{\mathrm{depth}}\,\Delta D_{\mathrm{depth}}+\frac{\theta_{\mathrm{cap}}}{I_{\max}\ln2}\,\Delta I_{\max} \tag{IV-3.9}
$$

**The exponential law of construction cost (Assumption A3.1).** Eq. (IV-3.10), with $\zeta>0$ dimensionless. **This is a modeling assumption, not a physical conclusion**: the Landauer bound is only a linear relation, and going from linear to exponential requires at least two additional assumptions — each new layer of rules requires all the old rules to be re-verified, or the error-tolerance margin shrinks exponentially with the number of layers; both hold in systems such as chip verification and airworthiness certification, and may not hold in software refactoring.

$$
E_{\mathrm{build}}^{(n\to n+1)}=E_{\mathrm{build},0}\cdot e^{\zeta C_D} \tag{IV-3.10}
$$

**Calibration route**: take the records of $N$ transitions within the same lineage, perform a least-squares fit of $\ln E_{\mathrm{build}}^{(n)}=\ln E_{\mathrm{build},0}+\zeta C_{D,n}+\epsilon_n$, and report three tests: $\zeta$ is significantly greater than zero; the residuals have no systematic trend; the span of $C_D$ within the sample is large enough. When $\zeta$ is not significant, Eq. (IV-3.10) does not hold, and $C_{\max}$ fails with it. After substitution the three conditions are revised as Eq. (IV-3.11):

$$
I_{\mathrm{orig}}(t)>0\ \wedge\ E_{\mathrm{available}}(t)>E_{\mathrm{build},0}\,e^{\zeta C_D}\ \wedge\ \frac{\partial V}{\partial C_D}\Delta C_D>L_{\mathrm{lev,min}}\,E_{\mathrm{build},0}\,e^{\zeta C_D} \tag{IV-3.11}
$$

**The affordable ceiling is not a dynamic destination.** Introducing the budget ratio $\rho_E\equiv E_{\mathrm{build}}/E_{\mathrm{available}}<1$ gives Eq. (IV-3.12):

$$
C_{\max}=\frac{1}{\zeta}\ln\!\left(\frac{E_{\mathrm{available}}}{E_{\mathrm{build},0}}\right) \tag{IV-3.12}
$$

This is a **constraint boundary**, not an **evolutionary destination**: an allowance does not imply consumption. "Stop on hitting the boundary" makes $\dot C_D$ jump discontinuously to zero at the boundary, which is a **forced truncation** rather than a smooth approach to zero — in the former the trajectory flattens near the boundary (can be warned of), in the latter it turns suddenly (hard to warn of). The dynamic supplement is Eq. (IV-3.13):

$$
\dot C_D=\eta_C\left[\pi_V\,\frac{\partial V}{\partial C_D}-\zeta\,E_{\mathrm{build},0}\,e^{\zeta C_D}\right] \tag{IV-3.13}
$$

**Premises**: $V$ is differentiable in $C_D$; $E_{\mathrm{build}}$ obeys Eq. (IV-3.10); $\pi_V$ is the conversion rate that converts viability potential into joules ($\mathrm{J^{2}/(bit\cdot s)}$), declared by the modeler and not derived from the model; this volume provides no independent estimate of it, so (IV-3.13) serves only as a modeling interface and not as a quantitative prediction; $\eta_C$ is the capacity adjustment rate ($\mathrm{rules^{2}/(s\cdot J)}$), finite; $E_{\mathrm{available}}$ is constant. This volume retains the "rule" as a counting unit in the dimensions: $C_D$, $\theta_{\mathrm{depth}}$, $\theta_{\mathrm{cap}}$ are counted in rules, and $\zeta$ in $1/$rules; the counting unit is used only for checking and is not mixed with physical dimensions. **Result**: the two terms inside the brackets both have unit $\mathrm{J/rules}$, and both sides are in rules/s; the steady state is given by $\pi_V\,\partial V/\partial C_D=\zeta E_{\mathrm{build},0}e^{\zeta C_D}$, in three cases. **Assumptions relied on**: complexity evolves along a single scalar direction; marginal returns are monotonically non-increasing. **Failure conditions**: when marginal returns are non-monotonic there can be multiple steady states.

| Case | Marginal condition | Trajectory destination | Relation to $C_{\max}$ |
| :---------- | :------------------ | :---------------- | :----------- |
| (a) Interior equilibrium | The marginal return equals the cost at $C_D^{*}<C_{\max}$ | Stops at $C_D^{*}$, with $\dot C_D\to0$ converging exponentially | Unrelated; the budget has slack |
| (b) Boundary truncation | Within the feasible domain the marginal return is always greater than the cost | Truncated after hitting $C_{\max}$, with $\dot C_D$ jumping discontinuously to zero | Exactly equal to $C_{\max}$; unaffordable |
| (c) Contraction | Over the whole domain the marginal return is less than the cost | $C_D$ decreases monotonically | Far from $C_{\max}$ |

Case (b) is the true "hard-constraint lock-in," but it is a **boundary phenomenon**; case (c) is similar to case (b) in phenotype but opposite in intervention. Hence: **$C_{\max}$ is the boundary of the static feasible domain; only in case (b) does the trajectory touch it, and it touches it by truncation rather than by smooth convergence; in cases (a) and (c) the destination is unrelated to $C_{\max}$.**

### 3.3 Decoder–Environment Matching

$$
\eta_D=\eta_0\cdot\mathcal{M}(D,\mathcal{N}),\qquad \mathcal{M}\in[0,1] \tag{IV-3.14}
$$

$\eta_0$ is bounded by the Landauer bound as a theoretical maximum; differences in $\eta_D$ may come from $\eta_0$ (material, process, energy budget) or from the degree of matching, and in diagnosis the two must be separated. Environmental drift makes $\mathcal{M}$ evolve according to $\mathrm{d}\mathcal{M}/\mathrm{d}t=-\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\|\mathcal{N}-\mathcal{N}_0\|\mathcal{M}+\nu\dot D$, and the imbalance condition is $\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\|\mathcal{N}-\mathcal{N}_0\|>\nu|\dot D|$. This is the same microscopic mechanism for cognitive aging and organizational ossification: the mechanism is the loss of matching, and the capability may remain unchanged ($\mathcal{Q}_D$ may remain unchanged); the observable difference is "extraction gets worse even for familiar signals" versus "still good on familiar signals but no response to new signals." A system with high $\mathcal{Q}_D$ and low $\mathcal{M}$ can efficiently extract **wrong** information, which is the most dangerous state.

### 3.4 Triple Splitting, Decoupling Deviation, and Alignment Failure

**Path one: bounded convergence.**

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}}{\mathrm{d}t}=-\alpha_{\mathrm{L}}\,I_{\mathrm{net}}\,\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}+\sigma \tag{IV-3.15}
$$

$\alpha_{\mathrm{L}}$ ($1/(\mathrm{bit\cdot s})$) times $I_{\mathrm{net}}$ (bit) gives $\mathrm{s^{-1}}$, so $\sigma$ is the **deviation generation rate** ($\mathrm{bit/J}$) and not the deviation itself. The equilibrium deviation is $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}^{*}=\sigma/(\alpha_{\mathrm{L}}I_{\mathrm{net}})$; taking the tolerance $\Delta_{\mathrm{crit}}$ gives the **main criterion**, Eq. (IV-3.16): when $I_{\mathrm{net}}<\sigma/(\alpha_{\mathrm{L}}\Delta_{\mathrm{crit}})$ the equilibrium deviation exceeds the tolerance, and all three quantities have proxy indicators.

$$
\frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}^{*}}{\Delta_{\mathrm{crit}}}=\frac{\sigma}{\alpha_{\mathrm{L}}I_{\mathrm{net}}\Delta_{\mathrm{crit}}}>1 \tag{IV-3.16}
$$

**Path two: self-referential amplification (auxiliary model).** When the system models itself, an $I$–$V$ closed loop appears: $\dot I=(I_{\mathrm{bas}}(1+\chi V)-I)/\tau_I$, $\dot V=k_II^{\alpha_{\mathrm{eff}}}/V-\gamma_0V$. Linearized at the fixed point, $\operatorname{tr}J<0$ always holds, and stability is determined by the sign of $\det J$, that is, by the dimensionless loop gain $\lambda_{\mathrm{J}}=2-\alpha_{\mathrm{eff}}\tilde{\chi}$, where $\tilde{\chi}\equiv\dfrac{\chi V^{*}}{1+\chi V^{*}}\in(0,1)$ is the **loop gain** at the positive equilibrium $V^{*}$ of this system (the same term as the one inside the brackets of $\det J$ in Volume I (I-5.12); the two can be identified with each other from here on); the critical value is $\tilde{\chi}_{\mathrm{c}}=2/\alpha_{\mathrm{eff}}$, meaningful only when $\alpha_{\mathrm{eff}}>2$ — from $\tilde{\chi}<1$ it follows immediately that $\lambda_{\mathrm{J}}>2-\alpha_{\mathrm{eff}}$, so when $\alpha_{\mathrm{eff}}\le2$ **there is no saddle-node bifurcation** ($\det J$ does not change sign); when $\alpha_{\mathrm{eff}}=2$ the equilibrium disappears at $\chi=1/\sqrt{\kappa}$ (that is the boundary of the equilibrium, not a bifurcation); when $\alpha_{\mathrm{eff}}<2$ there is a unique stable positive equilibrium for any $\chi\ge0$. $\tilde{\chi}$ and $\chi_{\mathrm{c}}$ are not interchangeable: the former is dimensionless and $\in(0,1)$, and the latter has unit $\mathrm{J/(bit\cdot s)}$ and no upper bound. **$\chi_{\mathrm{c}}$ is not used as a criterion**: $\chi$ has no independent measurement method and can only be back-inferred by fitting, and fitting requires that amplification has already been observed, which amounts to predicting failure with failure. The two sets of critical parameters are not interchangeable: $\chi_{\mathrm{c}}$ is the absolute level of the self-referential coupling strength, and $\mu_{\mathrm{rec}}/\delta_{\mathrm{decay}}$ is the relative size of the growth rate and the decay rate. Diagnosis first looks at the time shape — path one converges to a plateau, and path two diverges exponentially. **Limitation**: this auxiliary model does not contain a source term for $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}$; Volume I Eq. (I-5.14) gives $\Delta^{*}\propto1/I_{\mathrm{net}}$ when $I_{\mathrm{net}}$ is constant, and its direction is opposite to "$\chi$ amplification leads to deviation amplification," so in this volume this chain is treated only as an auxiliary model awaiting verification.

***

## Chapter 4 — Coupled Evolution of Decoder and Execution Layer

"Strengthening execution only, without changing decoding" is a dead end, because three constraints bind the two ends together. The decoder and the execution layer form a flywheel with forced coupling:

$$
\begin{aligned}
&D\uparrow\ \Rightarrow\ I_{\mathrm{net}}\uparrow\ \Rightarrow\ \text{execution efficiency}\uparrow
\ \Rightarrow\ \text{environmental exposure range}\uparrow\\
&\Rightarrow\ \text{new}\ I_{\mathrm{orig}}\ \Rightarrow\ D\ \text{upgraded further}
\end{aligned}
\tag{IV-4.1}
$$

Every step has a breakpoint: the conversion of $I_{\mathrm{net}}$ into execution efficiency depends on the execution mechanism using information monotonically (it breaks when precision is locked by material limits); expanding the exposure range cuts both ways, bringing resources and also the risk of new mismatch; new exposure does not automatically produce $I_{\mathrm{orig}}$, and if the environment is homogeneous the new signal is zero; a new signal drives an upgrade only if the gate holds, otherwise it is "visible but unaffordable."

| Constraint | Criterion | Phenotype when violated |
| :----------- | :------------------------- | :--------------------- |
| Information constraint | The conditional entropy $H(a\mid s)$ of the executed action is significantly lower than the unconditional entropy $H(a)$ | Large action amplitude but random direction; net displacement tends to zero |
| Energy constraint | The marginal $V$ return is positive: $\Delta V/\Delta E_{\mathrm{causal}}>0$ | The more is executed the more is lost; "busy" on the books while $V_{\mathrm{obj}}$ falls |
| Time constraint | $T_{\mathrm{pred}}>T_{\mathrm{exec}}$ (execution period) | Actions are overwritten by the next one before their effects appear |

The three are **multiplicative** rather than additive: if any one is missing, the returns of the other two go to zero, so "a substantive improvement in execution capability must be accompanied by a decoder upgrade" holds only **within the region where all three are in force**; outside the boundary (such as a completely stable environment), pure execution detached from decoding can be sustained for a long time. The energy constraint requires a positive **marginal** return, whereas decisions are often based on the **average** return — in an established system the average return is high, and the new margin may already be negative.

***

## Chapter 5 — Cross-Decoder Games

### 5.1 Payoff Functions and the Prisoner's-Dilemma Structure

Two premises. **Payoffs are valued by each party's internal reading**: the four payoffs are subject $i$'s value evaluations of the four outcomes, using $i$'s own decoder reading; across subjects they are neither comparable nor additive, and game theory needs only the subject's internal preference ordering. **The single-round payoff does not contain the time horizon**: if one writes the payoff as $I_{\mathrm{net}}T_{\mathrm{pred}}/E_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and then solves for the critical value of $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$, what one obtains is an implicit equation.

The **common accounting window $T_0$** is a fixed time window prescribed by protocol, used to convert amounts of information into viability potential, and it does not vary with $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$. Because all payoffs have the same dimension and do not contain the endogenous time horizon, the critical horizon has an explicit solution. The strategy space is $\{C,D\}$:

$$
\begin{aligned}
\Pi_{\mathrm{coop},i}&=\frac{(I_{\mathrm{net},i}+\Delta I_{\mathrm{coop}})\,T_0}{E_{\mathrm{eff},i}-\Delta E_{\mathrm{share}}}-\frac{I_{\mathrm{net},i}\,T_0}{E_{\mathrm{eff},i}}\\[3pt]
\Pi_{\mathrm{def},i}&=\frac{(I_{\mathrm{net},i}+\Delta I_{\mathrm{exploit}})\,T_0}{E_{\mathrm{eff},i}}-\frac{I_{\mathrm{net},i}\,T_0}{E_{\mathrm{eff},i}}\\[3pt]
\Pi_{\mathrm{sucker},i}&=\frac{(I_{\mathrm{net},i}-\Delta I_{\mathrm{stolen}})\,T_0}{E_{\mathrm{eff},i}+\Delta E_{\mathrm{defend}}}-\frac{I_{\mathrm{net},i}\,T_0}{E_{\mathrm{eff},i}}\\[3pt]
\Pi_{\mathrm{pun},i}&=\frac{I_{\mathrm{net},i}\,T_0}{E_{\mathrm{eff},i}+\Delta E_{\mathrm{conflict}}}-\frac{I_{\mathrm{net},i}\,T_0}{E_{\mathrm{eff},i}}
\end{aligned} \tag{IV-5.1}
$$

All four terms are in $\mathrm{bit\cdot s/J}$; $T_0$ is a constant and cancels in inequality comparisons — this is the key to making the solution explicit. The $\Delta$'s are, respectively, the cooperation information increment, the cooperation shared energy consumption, the defection grab, the amount grabbed, the defense energy consumption, and the conflict energy consumption; all are internal evaluations: the sign is usually reliable, and the magnitude is often unreliable.

**Prisoner's-dilemma structure.** The standard ordering is $\Pi_{\mathrm{def},i}>\Pi_{\mathrm{coop},i}>\Pi_{\mathrm{pun},i}>\Pi_{\mathrm{sucker},i}$. The first inequality expands to ($T_0$ cancelled):

$$
\Delta I_{\mathrm{exploit}}>\Delta I_{\mathrm{coop}}+\left(I_{\mathrm{net},i}+\Delta I_{\mathrm{exploit}}\right)\frac{\Delta E_{\mathrm{share}}}{E_{\mathrm{eff},i}} \tag{IV-5.2}
$$

Derivation: $\Pi_{\mathrm{def}}-\Pi_{\mathrm{coop}}=T_0[(I+\Delta I_e)/E-(I+\Delta I_c)/(E-\Delta E_s)]$; after bringing to a common denominator the numerator is $E(\Delta I_e-\Delta I_c)-\Delta E_s(I+\Delta I_e)$, the denominator is positive, and the sign is determined by the numerator. The second term on the right grows as the share of the shared energy consumption grows, which gives a counterintuitive conclusion: **the more energy-saving the sharing in cooperation, the harder it is for defection to dominate** — when $\Delta E_{\mathrm{share}}$ is large, cooperation lowers the denominator substantially, and defection, although it raises the numerator, gives up the reduction of the denominator. When $\Pi_{\mathrm{def},i}\le\Pi_{\mathrm{coop},i}$ the structure collapses and cooperation becomes a dominant strategy, but **this shows only that "cooperation may dominate," not that "cooperation necessarily arises."**

| Ordering | Form | Equilibrium (single round) |
| :-------------- | :------------------ | :------------------------- |
| $\Pi_{\mathrm{def}}>\Pi_{\mathrm{coop}}>\Pi_{\mathrm{pun}}>\Pi_{\mathrm{sucker}}$ | Prisoner's dilemma | Both defect, Pareto-inferior |
| $\Pi_{\mathrm{coop}}>\Pi_{\mathrm{def}}>\Pi_{\mathrm{pun}}>\Pi_{\mathrm{sucker}}$ | Cooperation dominant | Both cooperate, no punishment needed |
| $\Pi_{\mathrm{def}}>\Pi_{\mathrm{coop}}>\Pi_{\mathrm{sucker}}>\Pi_{\mathrm{pun}}$ | Game of chicken | One yields, the other advances |
| $\Pi_{\mathrm{pun}}>\Pi_{\mathrm{coop}}$ | Conflict dominant | Both in conflict; extreme form |

All the orderings are arranged by the payoffs of a single subject, so the discrimination requires only that each report its own ordering — a preference order is easier to obtain than a preference value. The estimation errors have systematic directions: $\Delta I_{\mathrm{exploit}}$ is often overestimated, $\Delta E_{\mathrm{share}}$ is often underestimated, and $\Pi_{\mathrm{pun}}$ is often overestimated; the three directions agree, and all make $T_{\mathrm{crit}}$ overestimated, so the parties think that "the horizon is not long enough" and tend to defect early. (To retain the narrative that "the parties overestimate the horizon," the bias must be changed to the opposite direction: the defection gain underestimated and the punishment underestimated. Also: if $\Pi_{\mathrm{coop}}\le\Pi_{\mathrm{pun}}$, then $\delta_c\ge1$ and $T_{\mathrm{crit}}$ is undefined, which belongs to case (iii) of §5.2.)

***

### 5.2 Repeated Games: The Explicit Solution for the Critical Horizon (**Proposition**)

The discount factor of an infinitely repeated game is generated by the time horizon:

$$
\delta_{\mathrm{disc}}=e^{-t_{\mathrm{dec}}/T_{\mathrm{pred}}}
$$

$t_{\mathrm{dec}}$ is the decision cycle ($\mathrm{s}$), and $\delta_{\mathrm{disc}}$ is dimensionless. **The payoffs do not contain $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$**, so the critical condition is explicit in $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$. Under a grim trigger (permanent mutual punishment after one defection), cooperation is sustainable if and only if

$$
\frac{\Pi_{\mathrm{coop},i}}{1-\delta_{\mathrm{disc}}}\ \ge\ \Pi_{\mathrm{def},i}+\frac{\delta_{\mathrm{disc}}\,\Pi_{\mathrm{pun},i}}{1-\delta_{\mathrm{disc}}} \tag{IV-5.3}
$$

The left side is the present value of cooperating forever, and the right side is the present value of "defect this round, punished forever after." Rearranging gives $\Pi_{\mathrm{coop},i}-\Pi_{\mathrm{pun},i}\ge(1-\delta_{\mathrm{disc}})(\Pi_{\mathrm{def},i}-\Pi_{\mathrm{pun},i})$, that is, $\delta_{\mathrm{disc}}\ge(\Pi_{\mathrm{def},i}-\Pi_{\mathrm{coop},i})/(\Pi_{\mathrm{def},i}-\Pi_{\mathrm{pun},i})\equiv\delta_{\mathrm{c}}$, and taking logarithms gives

$$
T_{\mathrm{crit}}=\frac{t_{\mathrm{dec}}}{\ln\left(\dfrac{\Pi_{\mathrm{def},i}-\Pi_{\mathrm{pun},i}}{\Pi_{\mathrm{def},i}-\Pi_{\mathrm{coop},i}}\right)} \tag{IV-5.4}
$$

Dimensions: the numerator is in $\mathrm{s}$ and the denominator is dimensionless, so $T_{\mathrm{crit}}$ is in $\mathrm{s}$.

**Existence conditions.** Eq. (IV-5.4) gives a positive finite value only when the denominator is positive and $\delta_{\mathrm{c}}<1$, so three cases must be stated:

| Case | Payoff condition | $\delta_{\mathrm{c}}$ | Result |
| :---------------- | :---------- | :------ | :------------------------- |
| (i) Cooperation dominant round by round | $\Pi_{\mathrm{coop}}\ge\Pi_{\mathrm{def}}$ | $\le0$ | Holds for every $T_{\mathrm{pred}}>0$; $T_{\mathrm{crit}}$ does not exist (not needed) |
| (ii) Dilemma but punishable | $\Pi_{\mathrm{def}}>\Pi_{\mathrm{coop}}>\Pi_{\mathrm{pun}}$ | $0<\delta_{\mathrm{c}}<1$ | $T_{\mathrm{crit}}>0$ finite; cooperation if and only if $T_{\mathrm{pred}}\ge T_{\mathrm{crit}}$ |
| (iii) Insufficient punishment | $\Pi_{\mathrm{coop}}\le\Pi_{\mathrm{pun}}$ | $\ge1$ | No solution; under any finite horizon the grim trigger does not constitute an equilibrium |

In case (iii), **cooperation is not an equilibrium however long the horizon**: the gain from defecting once exceeds the difference between cooperating forever and being punished forever, and the real-world counterpart is "unenforceable punishment" — it exists on paper and poses no threat in terms of payoffs. $T_{\mathrm{min}}$ is a finite positive number, and $\delta_{\mathrm{disc}}\to e^{-t_{\mathrm{dec}}/T_{\mathrm{min}}}$ is positive and not zero, so the correct statement is "defection is an equilibrium $\iff\delta_{\mathrm{disc}}<\delta_{\mathrm{c}}$," not "defect when $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$ is small."

**The correct statement of the result.** Under the four conditions of **complete monitoring**, **grim trigger**, **stationary payoffs**, and **enforceable punishment**, cooperation is a subgame-perfect equilibrium if and only if $T_{\mathrm{pred}}\ge T_{\mathrm{crit}}$ and the payoffs satisfy case (ii). Each of the four conditions corresponds to one failure: without monitoring, the game degrades to "until detected"; without the trigger, the threat is not credible; without stationarity, the geometric series fails; without enforceability, the result is case (iii).

**Inference (Candidate Corollary).** If a subject enters the myopic runaway regime and $T_{\mathrm{min}}<T_{\mathrm{crit}}$, the repeated game it takes part in degenerates into a single-round prisoner's dilemma, and $\Delta E_{\mathrm{conflict}}$ rises; case-by-case testing is needed, and when the punishment is heavy enough, myopia may also sustain cooperation.

### 5.3 Conquest Games: A Two-Layer Structure of Zero-Sum Share and Negative-Sum Cost (**Proposition**)

The expansion of one party's $D$ comes at the cost of the compression of the other party's PS (an $I^{-}$ cascade): a subject with high execution capability enlarges the coverage of its own $I_{\mathrm{net}}$ and compresses the opponent's $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$ and $E_{\max}$. In the short term the conqueror's $V$ rises, and in the long term it may pay a high $E_{\mathrm{causal}}$ because of the resistance of the $D$ that remains in the conquered party.

**Why it must be split into two layers.** "Pure zero-sum ($\Delta V_A=-\Delta V_B$)" requires $\sum_i\Delta V_i=0$, while "both sides bear conflict costs" gives $\sum_i\Delta V_i<0$; the two cannot both be true. The contradiction is conceptual: "share allocation" and "cost bearing" were treated as the same layer:

$$
\Delta V_i=\Delta V_i^{\mathrm{share}}+\Delta V_i^{\mathrm{cost}} \tag{IV-5.5}
$$

**The share layer is zero-sum.** When the total amount of resources is fixed, the transfer is pure redistribution, and the measurement convention is consistent, $\Delta V_A^{\mathrm{share}}=-\Delta V_B^{\mathrm{share}}$, so $\sum_i\Delta V_i^{\mathrm{share}}=0$ — this is the only legitimate place for "zero-sum."

**The cost layer is negative-sum.** The energy consumed by conflict (armaments, intelligence, mobilization) is not converted into any party's share but dissipated. Let the effective cost of each side increase by $\Delta E_{\mathrm{conflict},i}>0$; then $\Delta V_i^{\mathrm{cost}}=-V_i^{0}\Delta E_{\mathrm{conflict},i}/(E_{\mathrm{eff},i}+\Delta E_{\mathrm{conflict},i})<0$, and summing gives

$$
\sum_{i\in\{A,B\}}\left(V_i^{1}-V_i^{0}\right)=-\sum_{i\in\{A,B\}}V_i^{0}\,\frac{\Delta E_{\mathrm{conflict},i}}{E_{\mathrm{eff},i}+\Delta E_{\mathrm{conflict},i}}<0 \tag{IV-5.6}
$$

The derivation uses only one assumption: $\Delta E_{\mathrm{conflict},i}>0$ — any sustained confrontation requires a resource input, even if extremely asymmetric. Combining the two layers gives $\sum_i\Delta V_i<0$, so pure zero-sum holds **only in the limit $\Delta E_{\mathrm{conflict}}\to0$**, and this limit is incompatible with "both sides bear costs": cost tending to zero means confrontation tending to zero, and it is no longer a conquest game.

**Four accounting premises for cross-subject summation.** **Common boundary**: include only the directly participating subjects and their direct resources. **Reference protocol**: readings are taken against the same baseline. **Observation window**: costs are counted within the same time window — the conqueror at the front (immediate), the conquered in the rear (lagging by several generations). **Cost de-duplication**: costs borne jointly are not counted once on each side's books.

**Competition intensity and the evolution of the viability potential.** Define the dimensionless competition intensity $\Upsilon\equiv\Delta E_{\mathrm{conflict}}/E_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{base}}$ ($E_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{base}}$ is the effective cost without conflict); then

$$
V(t)=\frac{I_{\mathrm{net}}\cdot T_{\mathrm{pred}}}{E_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\mathrm{base}}+\Delta E_{\mathrm{conflict}}(t)}=\frac{V_0}{1+\Upsilon(t)} \tag{IV-5.7}
$$

When $I_{\mathrm{net}}$ and $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$ are unchanged and the cost grows, $V$ tends monotonically to zero: $\Upsilon=1$ lowers it to half the baseline, and $\Upsilon>1$ enters a collapse zone (the threshold must be calibrated within the domain). This formula is a **lower-bound estimate**: in high-intensity competition $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$ and $I_{\mathrm{net}}$ usually shrink as well.

**Why conquest still occurs.** First, **the share layer is asymmetric**: a negative total does not rule out a net gain for one side. Second, **the costs are mismatched in time**: costs fall at the front (current period), and gains in the rear (lagged). Third, **internal evaluation bias**: the conqueror's decoder is optimized for "identifying conquerable targets," not for "assessing the cost of occupation," and therefore systematically overestimates share and underestimates cost.

**Parameter conditions.** $J_{ij}<0$ ($J_{ij}$ is the coupling-strength matrix element: positive for cooperation, negative for competition, zero for isolation; see Appendix A.4); $\Upsilon$ high; $T_{\mathrm{pred}}\to T_{\mathrm{min}}$; the payoff structure is a prisoner's dilemma (case ii).

***

### 5.4 Cooperative Games: Alliances, Marriage, and Cultural Integration

Cooperation achieves a reciprocal rise in $V$ through the local alignment of decoders: the cooperating parties share part of $I_{\mathrm{orig}}$ and $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$, lower their respective $E_{\mathrm{causal}}$, and jointly enlarge the reachable state space. The key condition is the existence of a commensurable subset of $D$ (a common language, value anchors); otherwise it degenerates into pseudo-cooperation — formally exchanging information, but in substance each side uses the other's signals to reinforce its existing readings.

**Cooperative superadditivity (Proposition).** Superadditivity depends on a premise that has not been declared: **the amounts of information of the two sides can be added directly**. The $I_{\mathrm{net}}$ of the two subjects are each measured under their own reading protocols, and adding them amounts to assuming that the graduations of the two rulers can be joined; it fails in three cases — the classification of the same event differs, the information overlaps, or the observation windows differ. Therefore the addition must be marked as a modeling convention and a de-duplication rule must be given.

**Premises**: (P1) a shareable information subset $I_{\mathrm{shared}}\ne\varnothing$ exists, that is, the two sides can, under the same reading protocol, give consistent positive-effect or negative-effect determinations for the same batch of signals; (P2) the merged construction cost can be shared out, $E_{\mathrm{build}}^{\mathrm{coop}}<E_{\mathrm{available,A}}+E_{\mathrm{available,B}}$; (P3) the synergy term is non-negative, $\Delta I_{\mathrm{synergy}}\ge0$; (P4) the additivity convention: $I_{A\cup B}$ is computed under the same reading protocol with the overlapping information de-duplicated, and the common cost in $E_{A\cup B}$ is counted only once.

**Result**: under (P1)–(P4)

$$
I_{A\cup B}=I_{\mathrm{net,A}}+I_{\mathrm{net,B}}+\Delta I_{\mathrm{synergy}},\qquad E_{A\cup B}=E_{\mathrm{eff,A}}+E_{\mathrm{eff,B}}-\Delta E_{\mathrm{share}} \tag{IV-5.8}
$$

Take $T_{\mathrm{pred,A}}=T_{\mathrm{pred,B}}=T$ (or unify to $T_0$); then: the condition for **total superadditivity** is exactly $\Delta I_{\mathrm{synergy}}>0$, which is an identity; for **efficiency exceeding the no-synergy baseline**, that is, for $V_{A\cup B}=I_{A\cup B}T/E_{A\cup B}$ to be greater than $(I_{\mathrm{net,A}}+I_{\mathrm{net,B}})T/(E_{\mathrm{eff,A}}+E_{\mathrm{eff,B}})$, the condition is that at least one of $\Delta I_{\mathrm{synergy}}>0$ and $\Delta E_{\mathrm{share}}>0$ holds strictly; for **rationality for the higher-efficiency side**, let $I_{\mathrm{net,A}}/E_{\mathrm{eff,A}}>I_{\mathrm{net,B}}/E_{\mathrm{eff,B}}$; then $V_{A\cup B}>V_A$ if and only if

$$
\Delta I_{\mathrm{synergy}}\,E_{\mathrm{eff,A}}+I_{\mathrm{net,A}}\,\Delta E_{\mathrm{share}}>E_{\mathrm{eff,A}}E_{\mathrm{eff,B}}\left(\frac{I_{\mathrm{net,A}}}{E_{\mathrm{eff,A}}}-\frac{I_{\mathrm{net,B}}}{E_{\mathrm{eff,B}}}\right) \tag{IV-5.9}
$$

Derivation: cross-multiplying $V_{A\cup B}>V_A$ gives $E_A(I_A+I_B+\Delta I_{\mathrm{syn}})>I_A(E_A+E_B-\Delta E_s)$, and rearranging gives the result. **Failure conditions**: failure of any one means failure, but in different ways — (P1) degenerates into pseudo-cooperation; (P2) the merger is unaffordable; (P3) the merger instead lowers the amount extracted; (P4) the numerical values of Eq. (IV-5.8) are meaningless. So the conclusion is "under a unified protocol and de-duplication, $1+1$ can be greater than 2": in many alliances these two do not hold, and the phenotype is "beautiful accounts at signing, mutual blame at execution."

**Sustaining cooperation.** The horizon condition is the same as in Section 5.2: under complete monitoring, enforceable punishment, and payoffs satisfying case (ii), cooperation is a subgame-perfect equilibrium if and only if $T_{\mathrm{pred}}\ge T_{\mathrm{crit}}$. This is the dividing line between a "political marriage" and a "brief alliance": the former comes with long-horizon design, lengthening $t_{\mathrm{dec}}$ and pushing down the breach-of-contract $\Pi_{\mathrm{pun}}$, so that $T_{\mathrm{crit}}$ falls; but institutions can change $T_{\mathrm{crit}}$, not $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$.

**Identifying pseudo-cooperation.** The criterion lies not in gains but in the **direction of information flow**: in true cooperation the $D$ of both sides drift toward a common subset and $D_A\cap D_B$ grows over time; in pseudo-cooperation each drifts in the direction of "being better at extracting from the other," and the intersection does not grow or even shrinks. There are three measurable indicators: **growth of the common vocabulary** (whether shared formulations enter decisions), **correlation of misjudgments** (tending to agree in true cooperation, opposite in pseudo-cooperation), and **structure of exit costs** (high for both sides in true cooperation, very low for at least one side in pseudo-cooperation). All three are structural quantities, turning "whether the cooperation is real" from a question of intention into an observable question.

### 5.5 Coexistence Games: Cold Peace and Pluralistic Balance

Coexistence is a stable form that is neither cooperation nor conquest: the two sides maintain relatively isolated $D$, keep a high $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}$ without triggering hot conflict, and limit the drift speed of $D$ by institutions or norms; when the horizon is long, the discount factor is close to 1, and the temptation of a single conflict is offset by the long-term cost. When three conditions hold together, coexistence is a Nash equilibrium: **sufficient resources** (basic persistence needs do not depend on the same scarce resource; if the overlap exceeds a threshold, the zero-sum share layer is activated and the game degenerates into conquest); **enforceable mutual deterrence** (the share gain from a unilateral deviation is smaller than the cost caused by the other side's retaliation, on the same principle as the enforceability of punishment in Section 5.2, except that the form of punishment changes from "returning to defection" to "escalating confrontation"); **low-frequency interaction** (the share of a single gain in the long-term total gain is small).

Coexistence is fragile not for lack of goodwill but because it is maintained by "deviation not being worthwhile," and therefore it depends on parameters. The most typical change is a leap in execution capability: one side suddenly gains an order-of-magnitude improvement, its gain from unilateral deviation jumps while the other side's retaliation capability is unchanged, and the deterrence condition is broken — a "sudden collapse of peace" is mostly of this kind: it is not that intentions have changed but that capabilities have changed. **Parameter conditions**: $J_{ij}\approx0$; $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}$ high but $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$ long enough; neither side's unilateral deviation can raise $V_{\mathrm{obj}}$ — $V_{\mathrm{obj}}$ is used rather than $V^{\mathrm{val}}$ because coexistence is a state that is "not worthwhile on the books but not enough to collapse."

When $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}$ is too large and $T_{\mathrm{pred}}$ is extremely short, the system enters a high-risk zone. **Mutual destruction** is the two sides sacrificing their own $V_{\mathrm{obj}}$ to destroy the opponent's $D$; mechanically, $\Pi_{\mathrm{pun},i}$ is pushed in the negative direction: the punishment is "losing everything," and by (IV-5.4) $\delta_{\mathrm{c}}\to0^{+}$ and $T_{\mathrm{crit}}\to0$ — the heavier the punishment, the easier cooperation is to sustain, and even a myopic party is more willing to cooperate. This limit is only a formal limit under Eq. (IV-5.1): $V_{\mathrm{obj}}\ge0$ gives a finite lower bound for $\Pi_{\mathrm{pun}}$, and a confrontation able to destroy both sides' $D$ would at the same time depress $\Pi_{\mathrm{coop}}$, so that case (ii) fails first — what mutual destruction destroys is not patience but the payoff of cooperation itself. **Compromise** is controlled drift by one or both sides, partially accepting elements of the other's $D$ in exchange for peace; mechanically, $\Pi_{\mathrm{sucker},i}>\Pi_{\mathrm{pun},i}$: the loss from being betrayed is smaller than the loss from all-out confrontation; as $T_{\mathrm{pred}}\to T_{\mathrm{min}}$ the window for compromise closes quickly, because the gain of compromise lies in the future and its cost in the present.

Three quantities can appear before extremization: $w_{\mathrm{s}}\to0$ (in decision discourse the frequency of "survival" falls and the frequency of "principle," "dignity," and "historical standing" rises, a measurable linguistic proxy); $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}/\Delta_{\mathrm{crit}}\gg1$ (the two sides' judgment of "who is winning" seriously disagrees with the third-party accounts); the relative magnitude of $\Pi_{\mathrm{pun}}$ (if both sides estimate "all-out confrontation" as more acceptable than "being dominated," then $\delta_{\mathrm{c}}\to1$). **Parameter conditions**: $\Delta_{\mathrm{VV}}\gg\Delta_{\mathrm{crit}}$; $T_{\mathrm{pred}}\to T_{\mathrm{min}}$; $w_{\mathrm{s}}\to0$. What this section describes is a **structural risk**, not a prediction about specific historical events: parameters change, and institutions can change payoffs.

## Chapter 6 — Alignment Engineering in the Age of AI

What form the alignment problem takes when the decoder of one party (humans) evolves much more slowly than that of the other (artificial intelligence); which conclusions are propositions and which are only design recommendations. The strength of the conclusions in this chapter is on the whole lower than in the preceding chapters, for a reason that lies in the object: the internal state cannot be fully observed, the payoffs cannot be fully verified, and the commitments cannot be fully enforced — these three "incompletenesses" happen to break the premises of all the game-theoretic conclusions of Chapter 5.

### 6.1 A Game-Theoretic Reconstruction of the Alignment Problem

Traditional alignment assumes that AI is an executor with a fixed $D$ and that the task is to write the objective function correctly.
